# Part II · Chapter 10: Model Unit Economics, Algorithmic Efficiency, and Open-Source Competition

## 1. Model economics cannot be separated from infrastructure economics

APIs, subscriptions, and agent services sit on top of a real variable-cost stack: accelerator time, HBM bandwidth, KV cache, storage / retrieval, networking, third-party tools, human review, cloud margin, and serving utilization.

AI software should therefore not be modeled by default as near-zero-marginal-cost SaaS.

The relevant unit is:

> **Revenue per completed workflow − total variable AI cost per completed workflow**

## 2. Algorithmic efficiency changes the demand function

DeepSeek-V3 first-party disclosures include:[^1]

- 671B total parameters;
- 37B activated parameters;
- 14.8T pre-training tokens;
- 2.664M H800 GPU-hours for pre-training;
- 2.788M H800 GPU-hours for full formal training.

Using the paper's US$2/GPU-hour assumption, 2.788M GPU-hours corresponds to approximately **US$5.576M of formal-training compute conversion**.

The 2.788M total includes the report's formal pre-training, context-extension, and post-training compute categories. The DeepSeek paper explicitly excludes prior research and ablation experiments from this cost figure; it should therefore not be read as the full enterprise R&D cost.[^1]

The important implication is that frontier capability is not linearly fixed to hardware input. Architecture, sparsity, routing, and memory design can materially change the resources required per unit of capability.

## 3. Algorithms can reduce memory cost directly

DeepSeek-V2 reports, relative to DeepSeek 67B:[^2]

- approximately **93.3% KV-cache reduction**;
- approximately **5.76× maximum generation throughput**.

These figures belong to V2, not V3.

Their economic significance is that the same HBM capacity can support greater concurrency, memory traffic per token can fall, and serving utilization can improve.

Lower unit cost, however, does not guarantee lower aggregate infrastructure demand. If lower cost causes token, agent, and workflow volume to rise faster, total demand can still increase.

### 3A. Price compression does not imply falling aggregate compute demand

Stanford AI Index 2025 provides a historical anchor: at the report's GPT-3.5-equivalent MMLU performance level, inference cost fell from about **US$20 per million tokens** in November 2022 to about **US$0.07 per million tokens** in October 2024—roughly a **280× decline**.[^3]

That comparison depends on a specific benchmark, model set, and time window. It should not be rewritten as "all model prices fell 280×." What it does establish is narrower:

> **The unit price of a benchmark-equivalent capability can compress rapidly; model-layer rent is not fixed.**

Stanford AI Index 2026 also reports further convergence among leading models on Arena-style evaluations, shifting more competitive pressure toward cost, reliability, and domain-specific performance.[^4]

This strengthens the chapter's core mechanism: **falling model prices and rising total infrastructure demand can coexist**. The decisive variables remain workload volume, resource intensity, and utilization.

## 4. Open source compresses model-layer rent before infrastructure rent

Open-source models broaden capability diffusion, private deployment, and model-switching options while putting pressure on API premiums.

Model-layer switching generally happens faster than replacement of accelerators, HBM, networking, packaging, or data-center infrastructure.

The market can therefore experience:

> **model price compression + infrastructure utilization growth**

This is why falling API prices are not equivalent to falling AI-infrastructure demand.

## 5. GPU vs. ASIC should be compared at System TCO

Google TPU, AWS Trainium, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA create alternative compute paths for internal workloads and model companies.[^5]

The comparison should not focus on accelerator-die price alone. It must include:

- workload compatibility;
- compiler/runtime maturity;
- HBM / interconnect configuration;
- cluster scaling;
- debugging / porting cost;
- effective utilization.

A theoretically cheaper chip can still produce a higher system TCO if migration, debugging, or utilization are materially worse. This is a **report-side system-TCO mechanism**, not a claim that the cited vendors disclose one standardized cross-platform TCO comparison.

## 6. AI software unit economics

At minimum, production-workflow economics should include:

> **Contribution Margin = Revenue per workflow − inference − retrieval/storage − tools/APIs − retries − human review − compliance/operations**

The metrics that matter include:

- cost per successfully completed task;
- gross margin after inference;
- revenue / productivity value per workflow;
- retention;
- retry / failure rate;
- human-review burden;
- utilization of reserved compute.

## 7. Anchor Buyers let model companies shape infrastructure formation

The largest model companies increasingly influence cloud, chip, and power investment through multi-year compute commitments, dedicated data-center projects, and long-duration capacity reservations.

A model company is therefore not permanently limited to passively accepting cloud pricing. At sufficient scale, it can become a demand anchor for infrastructure capital formation.

The same mechanism amplifies risk. If architecture efficiency rises, revenue underperforms, or workloads migrate elsewhere, long-duration capacity commitments can turn into utilization and duration risk.

## 8. Price compression and rebound

Algorithmic efficiency and open-source competition reduce the unit cost of intelligence.

Aggregate infrastructure demand depends on:

> **Net Infrastructure Demand = Unit Resource Intensity × Total Workload Volume**

If unit resource intensity falls 50% while workload volume grows 4×, total demand still increases.

Long-run analysis therefore must observe real workload, utilization, and business outcomes rather than training cost, benchmark scores, or token price in isolation.

Chapter 14 adds a third mechanism: **AI inheritance**. A large share of potential AI workload does not start from zero; it can be layered onto existing ERP, CRM, databases, cloud systems, APIs, digital documents, source code, and established workflows. Workload growth can therefore come not only from more calls per task but from **the penetration of AI into an already large digital installed base**. Long-run demand must consequently be read through unit efficiency, workflow penetration, automation depth, and execution frequency together.

## 9. Long-run competitive variables at the model layer

The most important economic variables for model companies include:

### Capability per Unit Resource

How much useful capability can be produced from a given amount of compute and memory.

### Serving Utilization

Whether reserved infrastructure can sustain high utilization.

### Model Portability

Whether customers can switch between models at low cost.

### Distribution

Who can acquire and retain workloads at low marginal distribution cost.

### Monetization

Whether model capability converts into durable revenue or measurable productivity value.

### Infrastructure Commitments

Whether long-duration compute commitments remain aligned with real workload growth.

The final variable links directly to the Anchor Compute Buyer mechanism developed in Chapter 13.

## 10. Current read

At the August 2026 evidence cutoff, the more defensible description from this chapter's cited evidence is:

- model-layer price competition and capability diffusion are real;
- benchmark-equivalent unit cost has fallen sharply in the cited historical series;
- open-source availability increases model portability and buyer options;
- **the sources cited in this chapter do not establish a confirmed decline in aggregate infrastructure demand as a consequence of those model-layer changes**;
- large model companies are using long-duration commitments to shape infrastructure buildout.

Algorithmic efficiency can become either a **demand interrupter** or a **usage multiplier** for infrastructure. Which effect dominates can only be determined through workload volume, utilization, and unit economics.

## References

[^1]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report,” model architecture and formal-training compute, https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437
[^2]: DeepSeek-AI, “DeepSeek-V2,” technical report, MLA / KV-cache and throughput results, https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.04434 ; official repository, https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V2
[^3]: Stanford HAI, “2025 AI Index Report — Research and Development,” inference-price comparison at a GPT-3.5-equivalent MMLU level, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2025-ai-index-report/research-and-development
[^4]: Stanford HAI, “2026 AI Index Report — Technical Performance,” frontier-model performance convergence and the shift toward cost, reliability, and domain-specific competition, https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report/technical-performance

[^5]: Google, Ironwood TPU, https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/google-cloud/ironwood-tpu-age-of-inference/ ; AWS, Amazon EC2 Trn3 UltraServers / Trainium3, https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-ec2-trn3-ultraservers/ ; Microsoft, Maia 200, https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2026/01/26/maia-200-the-ai-accelerator-built-for-inference/ ; Meta, MTIA 300, https://engineering.fb.com/2026/08/24/networking-traffic/mtia-300-meta-training-chip-built-in-nics/

# Part II · Chapter 11: NVIDIA's System-Orchestration Strategy Beyond Accelerators

## 1. NVIDIA extends platform power through orchestration, not ownership

NVIDIA's leverage does not depend on owning leading-edge fabs, HBM fabs, or the full data-center real-estate stack.

Its strategy is better described as:

> **interface control + reference architecture + software/runtime + supply orchestration + financing coordination**

The system boundary now spans GPU / CPU, NVLink and switching, networking, rack-scale systems, liquid-cooling architecture, CUDA/runtime, AI Enterprise, NIM / NeMo, DGX, and cloud-service extensions.[^1][^2][^3][^4]

**Scope note:** Product, filing, and transaction facts are footnoted. Unfootnoted statements in the sections below are report-side mechanism interpretations of those disclosed architectures and contracts; they should not be read as NVIDIA management claims or independently measured switching-cost estimates.

## 2. Rack-scale reference architecture changes OEM / ODM economics

At rack scale, NVIDIA's disclosed system architecture couples accelerators, switching, networking, power/cooling, and software more tightly than a standalone add-in accelerator product.[^4]

The report's inference is that this narrows some degrees of freedom in whole-system design and qualification for OEMs / ODMs. That does not imply that Dell, HPE, Lenovo, ODMs, or other integrators lose product capability.

## 3. Networking is part of the compute platform

Large-model training and inference increasingly depend on multi-GPU scaling and collective communication.

When accelerators, NVLink/NVSwitch, InfiniBand/Ethernet networking, and the software communication stack are co-designed, the network stops being merely an external component and becomes part of system performance and switching cost.

Analytically, this shifts competition away from a standalone-accelerator comparison toward a more integrated AI Factory architecture.

## 4. DGX Cloud is a platform-extension channel

Without a separately disclosed large-scale revenue line, DGX Cloud should not be presented as a second business that has already replaced hyperscalers.

DGX Cloud / Lepton provides a controlled channel for exposing NVIDIA software, infrastructure services, and a compute marketplace across external capacity.[^3] In this report, it is therefore treated as a **platform-extension and deployment channel**, not as evidence of a separately disclosed hyperscaler-scale revenue business.

## 5. Software deepens integration and migration cost

Software products extend the system platform from hardware purchase into model serving, deployment, optimization, enterprise support, and lifecycle management.

Until independent software revenue is disclosed, this report does not estimate a fixed "software tax" or separate software profit pool.

The report treats the strategic mechanism as:

> **lower deployment friction inside the platform can increase the relative cost of moving production workloads outside it.**

This is an analytical switching-cost mechanism, not a separately disclosed NVIDIA migration-cost metric.

## 6. Asset-light orchestration raises capital efficiency

NVIDIA leaves much of the manufacturing CapEx with external foundries, memory vendors, packaging partners, and infrastructure providers while using long-duration commitments and prepayments to support future supply access. NVIDIA's FY26 Form 10-K provides the company-level factual anchor for this supply-orchestration model through its manufacturing, supply, and capacity commitments.[^4]

The structure is:

> system definition / cash generation stays with the platform → heavy manufacturing CapEx stays with suppliers → orders, prepayments, and commitments support future resource access

This raises platform capital efficiency and turns external supply elasticity into part of platform strategy.

## 6A. Asset-light does not mean zero balance-sheet exposure

Transactions disclosed in August 2026 show NVIDIA's supply orchestration extending partly into infrastructure capital.

- The company announced compute-financing platforms with large long-duration capital providers, targeting **more than US$500B of third-party capital mobilization over time**.[^5]
- In PORTS-Pike, NVIDIA is providing credit support for land + power + shell buildout and investing **US$1.5B** in SB Energy.[^6]

NVIDIA has not become a traditional heavy-asset operator that owns every fab, data center, and power asset. But an "asset-light platform" can no longer be interpreted as having zero infrastructure-financing exposure.

A more precise description is:

> **The platform seeks to keep fabs, data centers, and power assets on external balance sheets while selectively using cash, equity, credit support, and long-duration coordination to accelerate formation of critical infrastructure.**

That can improve Time-to-Capacity, but it can also pull customer-concentration, credit, and duration risk partly back onto the platform.

## 7. Physical AI is an extension path, not current Data Center economics

Automotive, robotics, simulation, and edge deployment follow the same system logic: hardware architecture, runtime, simulation, developer tooling, and model deployment form a combined platform.

But "strategically important" does not mean "already contributing Data Center-scale revenue."

Physical AI must be evaluated independently through design wins, deployment scale, revenue, reliability, and ecosystem adoption.

## 8. Boundaries to vertical expansion

NVIDIA's system expansion faces real counterforces:

- hyperscaler custom system stacks;
- open compiler/runtime and portability;
- alternative networking;
- customer multi-sourcing;
- regulation / interface remedies;
- physical power and supply constraints;
- China's parallel stack.

If these variables strengthen together, NVIDIA can remain the strongest system platform while losing some bargaining power over system definition.

## 9. "AI Factory" is a system-design concept, not an ownership claim

AI Factory should not be interpreted as an ownership claim that "every data center is an NVIDIA factory."

It is better understood as a design philosophy in which compute, memory, networking, software, racks, cooling, and deployment are optimized as one production system.

Its industrial importance is that the sales unit, qualification process, supply commitments, and data-center engineering are pulled into one coordinated roadmap.

## 10. Current read

As of 2026, the clearest change is that NVIDIA has moved materially beyond the accelerator-vendor role toward a system-platform / AI-Factory architecture.

"Vertical expansion" and "the industry is fully controlled by one platform" remain different propositions.

The next question is whether system definition can continue to translate into long-duration workloads, supply commitments, and cross-generation customer retention with low in-platform migration cost.

# Part II · Chapter 12: The Second System Stack — How Hyperscaler Substitution Actually Works

## 1. The test is workload substitution, not chip availability

The strategic question is not whether a hyperscaler can design an ASIC. It is whether an alternative stack can **absorb production workload at an acceptable TCO, migration cost, reliability, and scale — and keep doing so across generations**.

Google TPU, AWS Trainium, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA therefore must be evaluated as system stacks:

- accelerator;
- HBM / memory subsystem;
- compiler/runtime;
- scale-up fabric;
- scale-out network;
- rack/pod;
- orchestration;
- production workload.

The report treats **cloud system-stack substitution** as an economic substitution test, not a separate theory. A benchmark win is not enough. The stack must survive production utilization, software friction, and workload-specific system economics.

## 2. Captive workload is the structural advantage

The structural difference between the major clouds and an ordinary chip startup is that hyperscalers simultaneously possess:

- massive internal workloads;
- data-center scale;
- compiler / software engineering;
- cloud distribution;
- capital;
- power procurement;
- custom-silicon teams.

They do not need to reproduce CUDA's entire external developer ecosystem on day one.

If an in-house system continuously lowers cost on a large captive workload, it already creates real economic value and generates production feedback for improving the compiler, interconnect, rack, and deployment tooling.

That produces a powerful learning loop:

> **internal workload → production feedback → system optimization → lower TCO → more internal workload**

## 3. Google: TPU is already a system stack

Google's TPU path spans multiple generations.

Ironwood further moves the product definition toward large pod/system scale, combining custom accelerators with Pathways runtime/software, high-bandwidth interconnect, HBM, and Google Cloud workloads.[^1]

Google's competitive unit is therefore:

> **TPU + compiler/runtime + network + pod + captive workload + cloud distribution**

If this system continuously lowers total cost of ownership for selected training or inference workloads, it can divert part of NVIDIA's platform profit even without becoming the universal development platform for the entire industry.

## 4. AWS: Trainium is a system, not a component

AWS Trainium has likewise moved from accelerator toward system.

Trainium3 UltraServers, the Neuron software stack, AWS networking/data-center deployment, and large cloud workloads together form an alternative system.[^2]

AWS also benefits from long-duration anchor demand. Anthropic has publicly deepened its compute relationship with AWS, giving Trainium a large workload base on which to iterate system economics.[^3]

But "having an anchor workload" is not the same as replacing NVIDIA's external ecosystem.

The variables that still need to be tested include large-cluster scaling, utilization, software maturity, migration cost, and external-customer adoption.

## 5. Microsoft: Maia matters only if Azure-scale economics close

The correct standard for Microsoft Maia 200 is not whether it supports every model on day one.[^4]

It is whether Maia can create a stable cost advantage on a sufficiently large Azure / Microsoft internal workload.

Long-run competitiveness depends on:

- compiler/runtime maturity;
- scale-up / scale-out capability;
- HBM configuration and supply;
- rack/data-center integration;
- high utilization;
- broader Azure deployment.

If those conditions hold across a large enough captive workload, Maia can divert part of external platform economics.

Becoming a general external substitute would require lower migration cost and a wider ecosystem.

## 6. Meta: specialization can substitute without generality

Meta operates very large recommendation, ranking, and generative-AI workloads.

MTIA 300's logic is not to become a general-purpose accelerator first.[^5] It targets the company's most important internal workloads and gradually organizes silicon, software, networking, and rack deployment into a custom full-stack solution.

The economic objective is:

> **give the company's largest workloads a lower long-run TCO.**

MTIA does not need to beat general-purpose GPUs across every workload to create value.

## 7. Substitution changes platform economics before it changes upstream scarcity

### 7.1 Platform-rent diversion

In-house systems can reduce external accelerator purchases, lower dependence on an external software/platform stack, improve procurement bargaining power, and keep more economics inside the cloud platform.

### 7.2 Upstream scarcity can persist

Next-generation custom systems still need advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, networking, and power.

Their expansion can therefore increase competition for the same upstream scarce resources used by NVIDIA.

The combined result is:

> **Cloud custom silicon can weaken NVIDIA platform profit while intensifying competition for TSMC / HBM / packaging / power.**

The two effects are not contradictory.

## 8. Cloud CapEx is not an NVIDIA-revenue proxy

Hyperscaler CapEx includes custom silicon, third-party accelerators, networking, memory, data centers, power, offices, fulfillment assets, and other categories. Fiscal periods, PPE definitions, and lease accounting also differ across companies.

Therefore:

> **Cloud CapEx ≠ AI hardware subtotal ≠ NVIDIA purchases**

Higher CapEx can support NVIDIA shipments while also accelerating systems designed to substitute for NVIDIA.

The four-cloud CapEx numbers cannot be compared directly with NVIDIA revenue to construct an "AI compute tax rate."

## 9. Algorithmic efficiency is a separate axis

Algorithmic and architectural efficiency directly affects system demand, but it is a different competitive axis from hyperscaler custom silicon.

Efficiency can:

- reduce compute / memory required for the same workload;
- lower serving cost;
- increase hardware utilization;
- stimulate additional token / workflow demand.

Efficiency gains can therefore reduce unit hardware demand while increasing aggregate infrastructure demand through demand elasticity.

DeepSeek training-cost, architecture-efficiency, and unit-intelligence economics are analyzed in [Chapter 10](part-II-ch10-model-economics.md). This chapter does not repeat GPU-hours or KV-cache figures, so the same evidence has one canonical home.

---

## 10. Evidence standard for real substitution

Single-chip benchmarks are not enough.

At least the following variables must be observed together:

1. mature compiler/runtime;
2. large-cluster scaling;
3. high utilization;
4. acceptable workload-migration cost;
5. sustainable HBM / packaging / power supply;
6. stable rack/pod deployment;
7. significant production workload share.

Success on internal workloads can already create real economic substitution.

But broader erosion of an external general-purpose platform additionally requires:

- cross-customer portability;
- cross-workload reliability;
- external developer adoption;
- persistently lower migration cost.

---

## 10A. Two thresholds: internal economics and external ecosystem

A cloud custom system can succeed at two distinct levels.

### Internal Economic Substitution

A custom stack already creates real economic substitution if, inside the hyperscaler's own or anchor workloads, it can:

- run reliably;
- sustain high utilization;
- deliver lower TCO than the external alternative;
- continue iterating across generations.

This level does not require reproducing the entire CUDA external ecosystem.

### External Ecosystem Substitution

To weaken a general-purpose external platform more broadly, the system additionally needs:

- external customer adoption;
- developer portability;
- broader workload coverage;
- low migration cost;
- stable commercial support;
- multi-generation compatibility.

Therefore:

> **internal success ≠ external ecosystem replacement**

Internal success can still materially change hyperscaler procurement, bargaining power, and long-run platform-profit distribution.

## 11. The likely equilibrium is workload segmentation

The long-run structure need not involve one side eliminating the other.

A plausible equilibrium is **workload segmentation**.

That segmentation must also distinguish **training topology** from **inference topology**. Frontier training is typically more sensitive to large-scale collective communication, scale-up / scale-out fabrics, and synchronization efficiency. Inference is more workload-dependent, with memory capacity / bandwidth, latency, batching, power efficiency, and cost per token often carrying greater weight. At the same time, inference does not mean networking becomes irrelevant: Google Ironwood still integrates a 9,216-chip pod with dedicated ICI, while Microsoft states that Maia 200 scales over standard Ethernet to clusters of up to 6,144 accelerators.[^1][^4]

The report therefore does not generalize frontier-training interconnect barriers to all inference workloads, nor does it assume that custom silicon / Ethernet makes large-scale inference network-insensitive. The correct comparison is **memory, network, latency, utilization, and TCO for the specific workload topology**.

### Areas where NVIDIA can remain stronger

- general-purpose frontier training;
- broad external developer ecosystems;
- heterogeneous enterprise workloads;
- workload portability across customers.

### Areas where hyperscaler custom stacks can be stronger

- massive internal workloads;
- repeatable architecture;
- vertically optimized training / inference;
- cloud-specific TCO optimization;
- captive distribution.

The boundary depends on software maturity, memory supply, interconnect, workload structure, and migration cost.

---

## 11A. System-orchestration rent is workload-topology dependent

The value of a general-purpose system orchestrator is not constant across workload mix.

For highly synchronized frontier training, large-scale collective communication, scale-up fabrics, failure recovery, and cluster-wide software coordination can command a high premium because system-level execution risk is expensive.

As a larger share of production shifts toward inference or narrower fine-tuning workloads, some workloads can become:

- more partitionable;
- more latency / memory / power constrained than collective-communication constrained;
- easier to place on vertically optimized captive systems;
- more sensitive to cost per token / verified outcome than to maximum general-purpose flexibility.

That can reduce the economic rent attached to a universal training-oriented coordination stack **for specific workloads** and increase the value of hyperscaler custom systems.

But the report rejects a fixed threshold such as “inference exceeds 80%, therefore orchestration power collapses.” Public evidence does not support such a universal cutoff, and large-scale inference can still require substantial memory systems, networking, scheduling, reliability, and fleet orchestration.

The relevant monitoring variables are therefore:

- production workload mix by training / inference where disclosed;
- custom-silicon share of captive production workloads;
- cost per token / cost per verified workload on alternative stacks;
- memory and network intensity by workload topology;
- migration cost and software portability;
- external-customer adoption versus captive internal use.

The strategic question is not whether inference “kills” system coordination. It is **which coordination layers remain scarce and monetizable as workload topology changes.**

---

## 12. Current read

As of August 2026, the most defensible description is:

- cloud custom silicon is already a real competitive force;
- the competitive unit has moved from custom chip to complete system stack;
- hyperscalers are competing across accelerator, software, interconnect, rack/pod, and workload economics together;
- frontier training and the broad external ecosystem have not been shown to have systematically moved away from NVIDIA;
- custom systems still compete heavily for advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, and power.

Therefore:

> **cloud system-stack substitution is one of the structural variables most capable of moving S2 — Baseline Concentration toward S1 — Competitive Expansion.**

Its strength must be measured through production workload share, migration cost, utilization, external adoption, and upstream-resource dependence—not product-launch count.

## 13. What would change the view

The cloud system-stack substitution thesis should be downgraded if custom systems remain confined to small internal workloads and the following conditions persist:

- production utilization remains low;
- migration / debugging cost stays high;
- external customer adoption remains weak;
- multi-generation software compatibility remains insufficient;
- HBM / packaging / power access does not improve;
- hyperscalers increase dependence on one external platform again.

The evidence that would genuinely support S1 is not another chip launch. It is simultaneous improvement in production workload share, TCO, portability, and external adoption.

## References

[^1]: Google Cloud, “Ironwood TPU,” https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/google-cloud/ironwood-tpu-age-of-inference/
[^2]: Amazon Web Services, “Amazon EC2 Trn3 UltraServers,” https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-ec2-trn3-ultraservers/
[^3]: Anthropic, “Anthropic and Amazon expand collaboration,” long-duration AWS compute commitment, https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-amazon-compute?lang=us
[^4]: Microsoft, “Maia 200: the AI accelerator built for inference,” 2026-01-26, https://blogs.microsoft.com/blog/2026/01/26/maia-200-the-ai-accelerator-built-for-inference/
[^5]: Meta Engineering, “MTIA 300,” 2026-08-24, https://engineering.fb.com/2026/08/24/networking-traffic/mtia-300-meta-training-chip-built-in-nics/

# Part II · Chapter 13: Anchor Compute Buyers — How Long-Duration Demand Pulls Infrastructure Forward

## 1. Frontier model demand can pull infrastructure forward

At smaller scale, a model company is primarily a compute tenant. At frontier scale, durable demand can become a financing input for chips, data centers, land, and power.

The relationship changes from:

> model company → rent existing compute

toward:

> **model-company commitment → revenue visibility → financing certainty → earlier cloud / data-center / power / chip buildout**

The analytical question is not whether a model company "owns the stack." It is whether its demand is large and durable enough to change when upstream capacity gets financed, reserved, and built.

## 2. Demand certainty becomes a capital-formation input

The mechanism can be written as:

> **long-duration model demand → infrastructure-financing certainty → earlier data-center/power buildout → earlier chip/HBM/packaging reservation → priority capacity for anchor workloads**

Model-company demand has become an upstream signal for capital formation, not merely a passive consequence of hyperscaler investment.

## 3. OpenAI / Stargate: delivery state matters more than headline scale

OpenAI announced Stargate in January 2025. The Stargate Project was described as a new company intending to invest **US$500B over four years** in U.S. AI infrastructure; that figure should not be read as an OpenAI-only funding commitment.[^1]

The long-run objective was later described as securing **10 GW** of U.S. AI infrastructure by 2029.[^2]

The status moved further forward in 2026. On **April 29, 2026**, OpenAI said Stargate had **surpassed the 10 GW secured-capacity milestone** and had added more than 3 GW in the prior 90 days.[^2] On **June 1, 2026**, the **1 GW The Barn** data-center campus in Michigan broke ground.[^3] OpenAI's announcement does not further specify whether that 1 GW figure is IT load, total facility load, generation capacity, or another power identity, so this report does not convert it into any of those denominators.

These states must remain separate:

> commitment / secured capacity / construction / energized capacity / utilization

More than 10 GW secured does not mean more than 10 GW is already energized and operating.

### 3.1 PORTS-Pike: anchor demand now reaches LPS and credit support

On August 17, 2026, NVIDIA disclosed its role in the PORTS-Pike Technology Campus with SB Energy in Ohio. NVIDIA is providing credit support to secure land, power, and shell (LPS) capacity; the initial deployment is designed for **4.25 IT-GW** of AI-factory capacity. OpenAI is the customer, while SB Energy will build, own, and operate the data center under a **20-year lease** to OpenAI. Phased capacity is planned to begin coming online in **2028**.[^5]

The significance lies not only in scale; the transaction structure has also changed:

> **Anchor model demand + system-platform credit support + infrastructure developer + long-duration lease**

This shows a system seller participating directly in the bridge between a model company's demand/credit profile and long-duration physical-infrastructure finance.

The state boundaries must remain explicit:

- 4.25 IT-GW is initial deployment **design capacity**, not 2026 energized load;
- future PORTS-Pike capacity is buildout, not current active compute;
- a 20-year lease improves revenue visibility but also raises customer-concentration and duration risk;
- secured land / power / shell does not prove final compute utilization.

On August 26, 2026, NVIDIA management further said on the FY27 Q2 earnings call that OpenAI's existing and planned NVIDIA Compute commitments total approximately **12 GW**.[^6] This is platform-side management commentary about demand visibility. It is not evidence that OpenAI already has 12 GW of energized capacity.

The relationship between PORTS-Pike's **4.25 IT-GW** and OpenAI's previously disclosed **>10 GW secured capacity** is not stated in public materials; this report neither adds the two figures nor assumes a subordination relationship.

---

## 4. Anthropic / AWS: anchor workload can accelerate a second stack

Anthropic disclosed in April 2026 that:[^4]

- its future ten-year commitment to AWS technologies is **more than US$100B**;
- it is securing **up to 5 GW of new compute capacity**;
- it is already using **more than 1M Trainium2 chips**;
- by the end of 2026 it expects close to **1 GW of Trainium2/3 capacity** to be coming online.

The nearly 1 GW end-2026 figure is a deployment milestone within the broader collaboration and should not be added to the up-to-5-GW figure as a separate capacity pool.

Model-company demand can become an important anchor workload for cloud custom silicon.

The public disclosure establishes scale. It does not establish a "largest customer" ranking without a disclosed workload-share denominator.

### 4.1 xAI / Colossus: the self-built variant

xAI is the case of a model company entering infrastructure construction directly. In its December 2024 first-party update, xAI said **Colossus** had become fully operational in **122 days** using **100,000 NVIDIA Hopper GPUs**, and that it planned to double the cluster to a combined **200,000 Hopper GPUs**.[^7] The case shows a model company directly organizing large-scale compute infrastructure instead of relying only on pre-existing cloud capacity.

This path is more direct than Stargate or Anthropic/AWS: rather than renting capacity through a cloud or a joint venture, the model company itself drove front-loaded capital formation in land, power, and chips. Anchor Buyers can guide upstream capital formation through more than one mode: long-duration leased commitments and self-built buildouts can coexist.

The xAI case is subject to the same state boundaries: Colossus going online is not full utilization, and an expansion plan is not installed capacity.

## 5. Anchor Buyers gain coordination power, not ownership

A model company can influence:

- demand certainty;
- chip / system selection;
- cloud roadmaps;
- rack deployment;
- power planning;
- infrastructure financing.

But it does not normally own the leading-edge foundry, HBM fab, advanced-packaging fab, or transmission grid.

Anchor Buyer power is an increase in demand-side bargaining and coordination—not full supply-chain ownership.

## 6. Long-duration commitments create switching costs

A model company chooses a full system composed of accelerator, HBM, interconnect, compiler/runtime, scheduler, rack/pod, and cloud operations.

Migration can require kernel adaptation, distributed-training changes, performance tuning, observability rework, and operational revalidation.

That creates infrastructure switching costs and helps explain why exit costs from long-duration capacity commitments can be much higher than those of ordinary SaaS contracts.

## 7. Anchor demand still depends on workload economics

If architecture sharply lowers the compute required for the same capability, a model company can reduce long-duration capacity commitments, improve buyer bargaining power, and increase utilization risk for infrastructure providers.

If lower unit cost stimulates workload volume even faster, long-duration commitments can continue to expand.

Anchor-buyer demand is a dynamic demand function, not a fixed exponential curve.

## 8. Anchor demand propagates through the infrastructure chain

Large-scale model demand can propagate across the full chain:

> **model demand → cloud CapEx / custom silicon → accelerator / HBM / packaging → data center → power → grid / generation**

AI-infrastructure analysis must look beyond:

- NVIDIA revenue;
- hyperscaler CapEx;
- foundry / memory CapEx.

It must also track independently:

- model-company long-duration contracts;
- secured capacity;
- project construction;
- utilization;
- commitment rescheduling.

If Anchor Buyer commitments keep expanding and are absorbed by real workloads, the capital-formation force behind S2/S3 strengthens.

If commitments are delayed, reduced, renegotiated, or underutilized, S4 risk rises.

---

## 9. Risk: concentration and duration mismatch

Anchor Buyers improve financing certainty, but they also concentrate risk.

If a large infrastructure project depends on a small number of model-company workloads, capital recovery becomes more exposed to those customers' future:

- revenue;
- financing;
- technical roadmap;
- workload growth;
- contract performance.

At the same time, AI hardware has a relatively short technology cycle while:

- data-center shells;
- power contracts;
- grid interconnections;
- generation assets

have much longer economic lives.

That creates a structural mismatch:

> **shorter-cycle AI workload / compute technology vs longer-cycle infrastructure obligation**

If the model-company growth thesis fails, a long-duration commitment can move from a financing anchor to a stranded-capacity risk.

---

## 10. Monitoring Anchor-Buyer strength

### Strengthening signals

- long-duration compute commitments continue to expand;
- secured capacity grows;
- dedicated campus / power projects move into construction;
- custom silicon receives more anchor workloads;
- utilization and workload revenue grow together;
- commitments are renewed or extended.

### Weakening signals

- project rescheduling;
- commitment renegotiation;
- cancellation;
- utilization below plan;
- model-company financing or revenue pressure;
- capacity delivery materially outrunning workload growth.

A headline commitment is not the endpoint of the analysis.

The decisive question is:

> **Does long-duration demand ultimately convert into high-utilization, monetizable production workload?**

---

## 11. Current read

As of August 2026, large model companies constitute a distinct Anchor Buyer category within AI infrastructure.

OpenAI Stargate's secured-capacity and construction milestones, Anthropic/AWS's ten-year compute commitment and Trainium deployment, xAI's self-built Colossus buildout, and PORTS-Pike's LPS/credit-support structure show that model workloads are now shaping:

> **cloud architecture, custom silicon, data-center buildout, power procurement, and infrastructure finance**

Anchor-buyer commitments are a key leading indicator connecting **S2/S3 expansion** with **S4 capital clearing**. The leading-indicator value of this signal is supported so far by three documented cases (Stargate, Anthropic/AWS, and xAI Colossus) and must be re-tested across additional full cycles.

They can accelerate AI-infrastructure capital formation, but under weak utilization they can also amplify stranded-capacity and duration-mismatch risk.

## 12. What would change the view

The anchor-buyer mechanism should be downgraded if:

- long-duration commitments are broadly delayed, reduced, or renegotiated;
- secured capacity fails for extended periods to become energized / utilized capacity;
- anchor-workload utilization remains persistently weak;
- model-company monetization cannot cover long-duration infrastructure obligations;
- project finance becomes increasingly dependent on stronger seller support rather than customer / third-party cash flow.

Conversely, larger headline contract values or additional secured GW should not, by themselves, strengthen this chapter's thesis without delivery and utilization evidence.

## References

[^2]: OpenAI, “Building the compute infrastructure for the Intelligence Age,” 2026-04-29, surpassed 10 GW secured-capacity milestone, https://openai.com/index/building-the-compute-infrastructure-for-the-intelligence-age/
[^3]: OpenAI, “Building the infrastructure for the Intelligence Age in Michigan,” 2026-06-01, 1 GW The Barn campus groundbreaking, https://openai.com/index/stargate-michigan-data-center/

# Part II · Chapter 14: Agent-Native Software and AI Software Unit Economics

> **Role in the report — demand-side validation module.** This chapter tests whether installed AI infrastructure can convert into repeatable, monetizable workflows. It informs demand duration and utilization, but it is not direct evidence of scarce-layer allocation and does not independently determine S1–S4.

## 1. The unit of software economics shifts from seat to workflow

Traditional SaaS can often add seats without proportional increases in variable compute. Agent-native software cannot assume the same cost structure.

A single workflow may trigger retrieval, reasoning, multiple model calls, tool invocation, browser/action execution, retries, and human review.

The cost unit shifts from:

> **account / seat**

toward:

> **workflow execution**

## 1A. 2026 evidence baseline: broad AI use, early scaled-agent deployment

Stanford AI Index 2026 summarizes organizational survey evidence collected in 2025: **88% of respondents say their organization uses AI in at least one business function**, while **79% say their organization regularly uses Generative AI in at least one function**.[^3] The underlying survey also shows that **scaled AI-agent use remains at or below roughly 10% in any given business function**, even though organization-level experimentation or scaling somewhere in the business is materially higher.[^3]

The correct reading is not a fixed penetration forecast, but a stage-of-market distinction:

> **Broad AI adoption has already occurred, while production-grade autonomous or semi-autonomous workflow deployment is still early.**

That creates a large potential workflow pool, but production reliability, review burden, integration cost, and unit economics have not yet been proven at the same scale.

Anthropic's June 2026 Economic Index also reports that its product usage has shifted toward more long-running agentic tasks and that higher compute use is associated with higher estimated artifact value.[^4] Because this is first-party product telemetry, it should not be treated as representative of the entire enterprise-software market.

## 1B. AI inheritance: IT is the installed base

Moving a physical industrial process into the digital domain usually requires sensors, networks, databases, software, identity systems, process digitization, and organizational change. A highly digitized organization starts from a different position: ERP, CRM, MES, CAD/PLM, databases, cloud systems, APIs, digital documents, source code, identity/access systems, and workflow logs already form a reusable digital substrate.

The report uses **AI inheritance** as shorthand for the structural advantage created when AI can add an intelligence layer on top of existing digital assets and workflows without rebuilding the full digital foundation.

The demand transmission is:

> **Digital Installed Base → AI Overlay → Workflow Penetration → Automation Depth → Workload Volume → Infrastructure Demand**

Long-run AI-infrastructure demand does not depend only on whether individual models keep scaling upward. It also depends on **how many existing digital workflows begin invoking AI and how deeply each workflow moves from assistant features toward semi-autonomous or autonomous execution**.

This mechanism is compatible with algorithmic efficiency:

- compute / memory required per task can keep falling;
- the number of workflows for which AI becomes economical can expand;
- execution frequency and agent depth inside each workflow can rise;
- aggregate infrastructure demand depends on whether **unit resource intensity falls faster or slower than workflow penetration and execution volume rise**.

### Boundary: lower Digital → AI friction does not mean zero-friction deployment

Existing digital infrastructure can reduce marginal deployment friction, but production-grade AI can still be constrained by:

- data quality / access;
- legacy integration;
- identity / permission;
- security / privacy;
- compliance / responsibility;
- workflow redesign;
- reliability / observability;
- human review;
- unit economics.

Physical AI and industrial processes that are not yet digitized still require sensorization, control integration, qualification, safety engineering, and field modification.

A more defensible statement is:

> **Relative to Industrial → Digital transformation, highly digitized organizations generally face lower marginal transformation cost when moving from Digital → AI. AI can inherit the IT installed base, but it cannot skip production integration or responsibility constraints.**

## 2. There is no universal AI-software margin structure

Variable cost differs by product and depends on model choice, context length, caching, retrieval, tools/APIs, failure/retry rates, human review, pricing model, and task depth.

A fixed path such as "traditional SaaS 85% → AI SaaS 55%" is not treated as fact.

The report uses **AI Software Unit Economics** instead.

## 3. Core unit-economics identity

A production workflow can be approximated as:

> **Contribution Margin = Revenue / Productivity Value per workflow − Variable AI cost − Tools/APIs − Human review**

Variable AI cost includes at least:

- inference;
- retrieval / storage;
- model routing;
- retries;
- observability;
- failure handling.

The optimization target is **cost per successfully completed workflow**, not merely cost per token.

## 4. Agent depth changes the cost curve

A shallow assistant may look like:

> single prompt → single response

A deeper agent looks more like:

> plan → retrieve → reason → tool call → inspect → retry → finalize

As loop depth grows, compute, tool usage, and the failure surface all expand.

Agent products must optimize:

- task success;
- loop length;
- model routing;
- caching;
- tool reliability;
- failure / retry rate.

## 5. Seat pricing comes under pressure

Seat pricing can remain effective for summarization, autocomplete, and shallow-assistant products.

If one seat can launch research, coding, support, sales, and operations workflows with very different depths, compute variance becomes much larger.

Long-run pricing can shift toward combinations of:

- usage;
- credits;
- workflow;
- outcome;
- hybrid models.

This does not imply that seat pricing disappears. It means agent depth weakens the correlation between a seat and real variable cost.

## 6. Multi-model routing becomes infrastructure

Sending every task to the most expensive model directly weakens unit economics.

A more efficient system design can use:

- cheap / local models for classification, extraction, and high-volume routine work;
- medium models for routine reasoning;
- frontier models for complex tasks and high-uncertainty decisions;
- specialist/custom models for stable professional workloads.

Routing is a dynamic allocation problem across quality, latency, cost, and failure probability.

## 7. Open source can move profit toward workflow and distribution

Open-source models increase portability, pressure API premiums, expand private deployment, and improve buyer bargaining power.

Model capability itself becomes harder to preserve as permanent software margin.

More durable value can move toward:

- proprietary workflow;
- domain data / context;
- distribution;
- integration;
- trust / compliance;
- measurable outcomes.

## 8. System-stack competition can transmit into application cost

Software companies usually do not purchase HBM, CoWoS, or grid capacity directly.

Cloud pricing, however, embeds accelerator, memory, networking, data-center, and power costs.

If hyperscaler custom stacks lower real system TCO, **application-layer benefit still depends on cloud pricing pass-through**. The cited Ironwood and Trainium disclosures establish alternative infrastructure systems; they do not by themselves prove that lower infrastructure TCO has already been transmitted into instance/API pricing or software contribution margin.[^1][^2]

The report therefore treats system-TCO → cloud-price → application-margin transmission as a **conditional mechanism to monitor**, not as an observed pricing fact.

## 8A. Workflow elasticity: lower unit cost does not imply lower total demand

Once agent software turns model calls into workflow execution, aggregate infrastructure demand can be approximated as:

```text
Total AI Infrastructure Demand
≈ Workflow Count
× Attempts per Workflow
× Resource Intensity per Attempt
```

Lower model prices or more efficient routing reduce only part of this expression.

If lower cost also produces:

- more users;
- longer agent loops;
- more tool calls;
- deeper retry / exploration;
- automation of workflows that were previously uneconomic;

then total compute / memory / network demand can still rise.

The software layer's feedback into infrastructure is therefore better summarized as:

> **cost per verified workflow × demand elasticity**

This is why agent adoption can compress model-layer margin while still expanding aggregate use of cloud, accelerators, memory, and networking.

## 9. Human review is a variable-cost line

In high-risk workflows such as legal, finance, and enterprise operations, human review may remain necessary for a long time.

If agent-generation speed rises 10× but review burden does not fall, labor has simply moved from production to verification.

True automation ROI should include:

> **Human time before − Human time after − new review / exception-handling time**

rather than measuring AI usage or generated tokens alone.

## 10. Sources of durable software profit

Products with stronger long-run economics are more likely to combine:

- high workflow completion;
- low variable AI cost;
- low retry / review burden;
- strong proprietary context;
- distribution;
- measurable customer ROI;
- the ability to route across models and providers.

Software competition is moving from feature competition toward **workflow economics competition**.

## 11. Current read

AI is moving part of software from low-marginal-cost replication toward real-time compute-consuming workflow systems.

This can compress some traditional software margins, but it does not imply that all software becomes a low-margin business.

The dividing line is:

> **who can convert model calls into repeatable, verifiable, low-failure, monetizable business outcomes while keeping workflow-demand growth inside sustainable unit economics.**

## References

[^1]: Google Cloud, “Ironwood TPU,” https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/infrastructure-and-cloud/google-cloud/ironwood-tpu-age-of-inference/
[^2]: Amazon Web Services, “Amazon EC2 Trn3 UltraServers,” https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/12/amazon-ec2-trn3-ultraservers/
[^3]: Stanford HAI, “2026 AI Index Report — Economy,” full Chapter 4 / Figure 4.3.1, based on McKinsey's 2025 organizational survey: 88% AI use and 79% regular Generative-AI use in at least one business function; the chapter-highlight webpage separately prints 70% for GenAI, so this report follows the full chapter figure/text rather than that inconsistent highlight, https://hai.stanford.edu/assets/files/ai_index_report_2026_chapter_4_economy.pdf
[^4]: Anthropic, “Anthropic Economic Index report: Cadences,” 2026-06-26, long-running agentic tasks and the relationship between compute use and estimated artifact value; first-party product sample, https://www.anthropic.com/research/economic-index-june-2026-report

# Part II · Chapter 15: Physical AI and the Real Bottlenecks in Robotics

> **Role in the report — extended physical-economy module.** This chapter examines one downstream domain where AI infrastructure, edge compute, power, safety, and manufacturing economics interact. It does not assume robotics is already a primary driver of frontier-infrastructure scarcity and is not a load-bearing input to the current S2 baseline.

## 1. Robotics is a system-closure problem

Physical AI must close perception, planning, control, actuation, power, safety, and mechanical reliability at the same time.

A robot's productive value cannot be inferred from model capability or edge TOPS alone.

A complete system must close:

> **model capability × sensor quality × control latency × actuator reliability × energy efficiency × manufacturing economics**

## 2. Cloud training and edge control live in different physical regimes

Large-scale simulation, policy training, and foundation-model training can run in data centers.

Real-time control is constrained by latency, thermals, battery, sensor loops, and safety deadlines.

Many Physical AI systems naturally follow:

> **cloud-side heavy training + edge-side compressed inference/control**

A roughly 120 kW-class DGX GB rack system and a robot's onboard power / energy budget belong to different physical pools.[^4]

## 3. Robot BOM is form-factor specific

Humanoids, AMRs, industrial arms, autonomous vehicles, and drones have very different actuator, reducer, sensor, compute, and battery cost structures.

Fixed templates such as "actuators 35% / sensors 20% / compute 25%" are not treated as industry fact.

The analytical task is to identify the **scarce component** for each robot form factor.

## 4. Mechanical precision can remain scarce after compute commoditizes

The software narrative can easily understate mechanical constraints. Illustrative engineering examples include frameless torque motors, harmonic reducers, planetary roller screws, bearings, and precision gearboxes.

**Scope note:** These examples are not presented as a sourced market-share or industry-wide bottleneck ranking. The report uses them to identify mechanical variables—durability, backlash, repeatability, noise, yield, maintenance, and cost—that can remain binding even when compute becomes cheaper.

The industry can reach a state where:

> **compute gets cheaper while high-precision mechanical components remain system bottlenecks.**

## 5. Sensor quality determines whether model capability becomes control

Robotic systems may require cameras, depth sensing, force/torque sensors, tactile sensors, IMUs, encoders, and other feedback channels.

Model capability becomes stable control only when calibration, latency, robustness, and failure detection are strong enough.

The strategic value of the sensor stack is not just BOM share. It is closed-loop reliability.

## 6. Edge-platform advantage is workload-specific

NVIDIA Jetson Thor and other edge-accelerator platforms compete across:[^1][^2]

- compute;
- software;
- power efficiency;
- developer ecosystem.

An edge system does not need to replicate data-center rack architecture.

But a platform that spans cloud training, simulation, and edge deployment can create cross-layer cloud-to-robot ecosystem lock-in.

## 7. Physical deployment can create more cloud workload

Even if one robot uses modest edge compute, a large fleet continuously generates:

- video / sensor data;
- simulation workloads;
- retraining;
- synthetic data;
- fleet analytics;
- model updates.

The feedback loop can be:

> **physical deployment → data generation → retraining → more cloud compute**

Robot adoption does not necessarily reduce data-center demand. It can create new training and inference workloads.

### 7.1 The robot becomes a new memory / storage demand pool

Micron's June 24, 2026 investor presentation provides a useful quantitative anchor that must retain its identity as a **company forward-looking view**:[^3]

- L2+ and above vehicles carry more than **5×** the memory and storage content of an average vehicle;
- Micron states that humanoid robots carry roughly **10×** the memory content of an average L2+ vehicle;
- the company expects a sustained, multi-decade robotics memory-demand cycle to begin in the latter part of this decade.

This is not industry-wide actual shipment data and it does not establish a universal "hundreds of GB of DRAM + several TB of SSD per robot" configuration.

What it supports is the mechanism:

> **Physical AI commercial scale ↑ → edge memory/storage content ↑ + fleet-generated cloud workload ↑**

Robotics can become both a new edge-memory demand pool and a new source of cloud retraining / storage demand.

### 7.2 Robotics is both an AI demand source and an industrial-electronics demand pool

Physical AI is not only a new source of AI-compute demand.

A robot also depends on:

- MCU / control electronics;
- sensors;
- motor-drive / power electronics;
- industrial memory / storage;
- edge accelerators;
- networking.

It can face two upstream constraint sets at once:

> **industrial-electronics supply pressure + edge-AI compute / memory pressure**

This is why robotics deployment cannot be inferred from model capability alone. Even if cloud training and edge models improve rapidly, lead times and supply continuity in MCU, sensors, actuator electronics, qualified memory, or other industrial components can still constrain mass deployment.

The reverse attribution error must also be avoided: an industrial-component shortage is not automatically caused by AI. Attribution still requires the shared-resource, product-mix, or allocation evidence defined in Chapters 3 and 21.

---

## 8. The robot-side power wall is battery / thermal

The data-center power bottleneck is grid / interconnection.

The robot-side bottleneck is battery energy density, runtime, charging, thermal constraints, and peak actuator demand.

Physical AI should be evaluated through:

> **Useful task output / Wh**

alongside continuous runtime, recharge time, thermal headroom, maintenance intervals, and field reliability—not TOPS alone.

## 9. The mass-production threshold is manufacturing

A demo can tolerate expensive parts, manual calibration, low yield, and frequent maintenance.

Mass production cannot.

Scale thresholds include component yield, assembly time, field failure rate, maintenance interval, supply-chain stability, and unit economics.

The industrial inflection point for Physical AI must be demonstrated through manufacturing and deployment data, not demonstration videos.

## 9A. The commercial threshold is capital payback

Once a robot moves from demo to production, the relevant economic test is not only whether it can complete a task.

A minimal relationship is:

```text
Economic Output
≈ Useful Tasks per Hour
× Uptime
× Deployment Scale
- Energy
- Maintenance
- Human Supervision
- Financing / Depreciation
```

A robot can be technically capable and still fail to produce sustainable ROI if:

- utilization is low;
- downtime is high;
- maintenance intervals are short;
- human supervision remains intensive;
- replacement / component failure is frequent;
- upfront CapEx is too high.

Physical AI and data-center AI share the same capital rule:

> **capability must ultimately be validated through utilization and cash flow.**

The difference is that data-center bottlenecks are concentrated more heavily in compute / HBM / power, while robotics adds actuators, batteries, mechanical reliability, field service, and safety.

## 10. China's Physical-AI stack differs from its frontier-AI stack

Physical AI depends heavily on motors, batteries, sensors, manufacturing, consumer-electronics supply chains, and system integration.

Those layers do not perfectly overlap with the HBM / advanced-packaging / leading-edge compute closure required for frontier AI infrastructure.

China can have stronger competitiveness in robot systems and mechanical supply chains than in some frontier-AI infrastructure layers.

This is a separate industrial path and should not be reduced to a single "AI chip capability" metric.

## 11. Current read

Long-run value capture can appear simultaneously in:

- edge compute / software platforms;
- actuators / reducers;
- sensors;
- batteries / power electronics;
- manufacturing / integration;
- cloud simulation / training infrastructure.

Long-run robotics competition is not fundamentally a contest over which model tops a benchmark.

The industrial test is:

> **who can convert AI capability into reliable, low-cost, mass-producible, maintainable physical work with sufficiently high utilization to recover capital.**

## References

[^1]: NVIDIA Developer, Isaac platform, simulation/training and robotics workflow, https://developer.nvidia.com/isaac
[^2]: NVIDIA, 2026-05-31, Isaac GR00T Reference Humanoid Robot, Jetson Thor onboard compute and Isaac GR00T platform, https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-open-humanoid-robot-reference-design
[^3]: Micron Technology, investor presentation, 2026-06-24, humanoid-robot memory-content outlook, https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2026/q3/Micron_Q3_26_Earnings_Deck.pdf
[^4]: NVIDIA, DGX GB Rack Scale Systems User Guide, rack power consumption approximately 120 kW, https://docs.nvidia.com/dgx/dgxgb200-user-guide/hardware.html

# Part II · Chapter 16: AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple — Differentiated Competitive Paths

## 1. Competitors contest different layers of the stack

AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple compete at different layers of the system stack and operate with different manufacturing access, workload bases, distribution channels, and capital structures.

The useful question is not whether they form a single anti-NVIDIA bloc, but:

> **Which source of system-stack power can each company realistically erode?**

## 2. AMD: the most direct data-center system challenger

AMD Instinct is one of the most direct alternatives in data-center acceleration. AMD's current official roadmap also organizes Instinct GPUs, EPYC CPUs, Pensando networking, and ROCm into rack/system-level solutions.[^1][^2] AMD's August 2026 newsroom release separately states that **ROCm.AI is now generally available with ROCm 10**.[^14]

The real threshold is not GPU design alone. It also includes:

- ROCm / software maturity;
- scale-up / scale-out;
- HBM;
- advanced packaging;
- system reference architecture;
- customer migration / production support.

AMD's competitive unit has therefore also moved from chip to system.

## 3. AMD is exposed to the same scarce-capacity access problem

The report treats AMD and NVIDIA as **structurally exposed to overlapping advanced-logic, HBM, and packaging supply pools**, but the public sources cited here do not disclose customer-level allocation shares for AMD.

When NVIDIA demand is exceptionally strong, AMD's access to incremental scarce capacity is therefore a **report-side mechanism to monitor**, not a documented claim that AMD has already lost a quantified amount of wafers, HBM, or packaging capacity.

If HBM / packaging supply elasticity rises, the constraints on AMD system expansion fall.

AMD is therefore a useful observable for **non-anchor-buyer access / residual capacity outside the leading buyer**, without requiring a new bespoke core concept.

## 4. Intel: product clock and foundry clock run on different timelines

Intel simultaneously carries CPU/product competition, accelerator efforts, foundry strategy, and domestic-manufacturing policy. Intel's 2025 Form 10-K shows Intel Foundry continuing to pursue an external-foundry strategy and states that Intel 18A entered high-volume production in 2025.[^3] Intel's June 2026 VLSI Symposium update separately states that **18A-P has entered risk production**.[^4]

In July 2026, ASML and Intel also confirmed that High-NA EUV had entered real high-volume logic production on selected Intel 18A layers.[^9]

The Product Clock requires short-cycle delivery of performance, software, and customer adoption.

The Foundry Clock requires long-duration progress in process technology, yield, customer trust, packaging, and high-volume execution.

Intel's recovery cannot therefore be established by a single product, process milestone, or fab. High-NA HVM is a meaningful manufacturing-execution signal, while external-foundry adoption, yield economics, and customer trust still require separate validation.

## 4A. Historical memory: concentration changes cadence control, not engineering causality

Intel's client-CPU history is useful only as a bounded comparison. It should not be rewritten as “dominance automatically causes toothpaste-style stagnation.”

Intel's **2016 Form 10-K, filed in 2017**, disclosed a lengthening process-technology cadence across 14nm and 10nm, with multiple product waves planned on the same node. Its 2018 Form 10-K then stated that 10nm volume production had been delayed from the second half of 2018 into 2019.[^10][^11]

The defensible lesson is:

> **When one technical/manufacturing path carries most of the upgrade cadence, process execution, architecture cadence, and customer replacement timing become more tightly coupled to that roadmap.**

It does **not** prove that market concentration caused Intel's 10nm delay. The filing itself identifies manufacturing, yield, and implementation challenges; engineering causality cannot be replaced with a market-structure story.

Competition also returned through new paths rather than simply making the incumbent's roadmap accelerate internally. AMD launched the first Zen-based Ryzen desktop processors in **2017**; Apple launched M1 in **2020** as the first chip designed specifically for Mac, creating a different vertically integrated client-compute path.[^12][^13]

The useful institutional memory is therefore:

> **When an executable second system is weak, the incumbent has greater power to define upgrade cadence. When real competition returns, it often arrives through a different architecture / manufacturing / system path.**

The analogy to 2026 AI infrastructure concerns **migration cost and system definition**, not today's product cadence:

- late Intel client computing combined a mature market with process delay;
- the 2026 NVIDIA data-center stack combines explosive demand, rapid roadmaps, and scarce-capacity allocation;
- the former is closer to “thin upgrade cadence”;
- the latter is currently closer to “buyers want the next generation but face migration and quantity-assurance constraints.”

This report therefore does **not** call current AI scarcity “another Intel toothpaste era.”

The longer-run risk is narrower:

> **If production-grade second system stacks fail to emerge for many years, today's control over scarce-layer access and migration could eventually become greater control over the cadence of progress itself.**

If AMD, hyperscaler custom systems, open/runtime portability, China's parallel alternatives, or other production-grade second paths scale materially, the historical analogy weakens.

---

## 5. Qualcomm: edge substitution changes cloud demand

Qualcomm's relative strengths are closer to smartphones, PCs, edge AI, low-power compute, and connectivity. Its 2026 materials continue to emphasize local agentic AI on Snapdragon X Series PCs and on-device generative AI on mobile platforms.[^5][^6]

It does not need to reproduce NVL72 to capture AI value.

If on-device model capability, memory efficiency, and local inference continue improving, more workloads can move from cloud to device, reducing part of cloud-inference demand.

Qualcomm's primary effect on AI infrastructure is therefore **demand-function restructuring**, not direct substitution for frontier training systems.

## 6. Apple: vertical integration changes workload placement

Apple vertically integrates silicon, operating systems, devices, application ecosystems, distribution, and a massive installed base. Apple Intelligence publicly combines on-device processing with Private Cloud Compute, making workload placement a core part of its AI system design.[^7][^8]

Apple is not a frontier-training system vendor, but Apple Intelligence can influence where consumer workloads execute between **on-device processing and Apple's Private Cloud Compute**.[^7][^8] The cited architecture materials do not establish a separate public-cloud placement path for Apple Intelligence.

If on-device capability improves rapidly, Apple becomes an important platform variable affecting cloud-inference intensity.

## 7. Leading-edge access does not justify invented customer rankings

Without first-party customer-share or ranking disclosures, this report does not treat a specific-year Apple "largest/second-largest foundry customer" identity as fact.

The important forward variables are whether Apple continues to obtain advanced-node launch capacity at the scale required by its product roadmap, whether AI/HPC absorbs more incremental capacity, and whether device-AI strategy changes wafer / memory mix. The first of those variables must be assessed from product / process disclosures rather than inferred from anonymized customer rankings.

Historical revenue share is not a substitute for layer-specific analysis of incremental scarce-capacity access.

## 8. Competitive pressure is multi-directional

**Table 16.1 — Four differentiated competitive paths**

| Company | Primary competitive direction |
|---|---|
| AMD | Data-center accelerator / system |
| Intel | CPU + accelerator + foundry |
| Qualcomm | edge / PC / mobile AI |
| Apple | vertically integrated on-device AI / workload placement |

**Source:** AMD, Intel, Qualcomm, and Apple official company/product materials.[^1][^3][^5][^7]  
**Note:** The table classifies competitive paths; it does not imply that the four companies operate in the same market or have directly comparable market shares.

NVIDIA therefore faces competitive pressure across multiple layers rather than from a single unified opponent.

## 9. What would raise S1 weight

- AMD scales a production system stack;
- Intel improves foundry and product execution together, increasing manufacturing/system diversity;
- Qualcomm / Apple move more high-volume inference toward the edge;
- open software / interfaces lower migration cost;
- HBM / packaging supply becomes looser.

These changes can raise S1 relative to S2, but S1 and S2 are parallel conditional states rather than sequential stages.

## 10. Current read

The incumbent semiconductor companies have neither disappeared nor formed a unified alliance.

A more realistic structure is: AMD competes directly at the data-center system level; Intel is attempting to repair both product and manufacturing clocks; Qualcomm and Apple reshape part of the demand function from the edge and device layers.

Whether they materially weaken dominant system-stack economics depends on production workload share, software portability, scarce-resource access, and customer migration—not on product-launch count.
