# Part II · Chapter 17: China's Parallel Stack — Production-Grade Substitution vs. Autonomous Frontier Closure

## 1. The analytical error is collapsing three different thresholds into one

China's technology position cannot be represented by a single localization rate.

Three distinct questions must remain separate:

1. **Can domestic consumer-computing systems ship at meaningful scale?**
2. **Can PC / workstation systems sustain software compatibility, performance, and commercial scale?**
3. **Can a frontier AI stack operate production workloads and continue advancing when critical external inputs are constrained?**

Evidence from one threshold does not close the others. A strong smartphone supply chain is not proof of frontier-AI closure; frontier-AI constraints are not proof that China lacks semiconductor substitution capacity.

For frontier AI, two separate tests are required:

> **Production-Grade Parallel Stack:** can the stack run real workloads with acceptable performance, scale, portability, supply continuity, reliability, utilization, and economics?

> **Autonomous Frontier Closure:** can that stack keep scaling across generations when critical external manufacturing, equipment, materials, software, or service inputs are constrained?

The distinction is strategic. Production-grade substitution can become economically important before full autonomous closure exists.

## 2. Consumer computing: commercial substitution can precede manufacturing autonomy

Smartphones and parts of consumer computing can form a more complete domestic chain because they can draw on local SoCs, foundries, LPDDR/NAND, packaging, device manufacturing, operating-system/application ecosystems, and large domestic demand.

TSMC's 2025 platform mix still shows smartphone at **29%** of annual platform revenue and HPC at **58%**.[^1]

### 2.1 Shipment scale is broader than one vendor

Commercial substitution is broader than one vendor.

Huawei / HiSilicon has the more mature **premium in-house device + SoC + software/ecosystem vertical loop**, while UNISOC and Xiaomi represent separate domestic design and product paths. The evidence should therefore be read as evidence of multiple commercial design loops, not as a single comparable market-share series.

Xiaomi has also moved beyond a pure demonstration stage. Xinhua reported in May 2025 that XRing O1 had entered mass production in the Xiaomi 15S Pro and Pad 7 Ultra.[^3] Reuters reported in August 2026 that O1 had powered **more than 1M devices**. The same Reuters report, citing people familiar with the matter, said the next-generation O3 was planned for TSMC 3 nm; one source said shipments for an initial foldable-phone application were targeted at **200k–300k units**.[^4]

The claim that Xiaomi has only "200k–300k scraps of capacity," or that the program is merely a branding exercise, therefore does not enter the fact layer.

A better judgment is:

> **Xiaomi has achieved real production deployment, while multi-generation scale, modem/system integration, foundry dependency, and sustained shipment share still require validation.**

UNISOC represents a different commercial loop in mass-market and 5G device silicon without proving premium-flagship system equivalence.

### 2.2 Design origin is not manufacturing closure

One additional supply-chain discipline is required here:

> **"Domestic design" does not mean "manufacturing autonomy," and shipment share cannot substitute for foundry continuity.**

UNISOC is a fabless IC-design company. Its public materials support a narrower manufacturing statement than a blanket "6 nm EUV smartphone SoC" claim: the M6870 high-end display / VR platform explicitly identifies **TSMC 6 nm EUV**, while the T9100 smartphone SoC specifies a **6 nm process** but does not identify the foundry or state that the process is EUV.[^19][^21] UNISOC / Spreadtrum also has a long first-party manufacturing history with TSMC: in 2011, TSMC disclosed that a Spreadtrum 40 nm baseband processor had entered initial production at TSMC Fab 12.[^20]

Those observations do **not** support the stronger claim that "all UNISOC products are manufactured by TSMC." The safe conclusion is therefore:

> **UNISOC demonstrates real commercial design capability, but public product evidence does not establish domestic foundry closure. Foundry identity and continuity must be evaluated as a separate gate.**

The Xiaomi XRing case is more direct: the Reuters source already cited in this chapter explicitly reports TSMC manufacturing cooperation.[^4] Xiaomi's in-house SoC program is therefore also a case where design capability is real but manufacturing autonomy cannot be inferred from the design origin.

This changes the consumer-computing test. Any future "domestic SoC share" should be decomposed into at least:

1. **design origin**;
2. **foundry identity / process continuity**;
3. **packaging / memory dependency**;
4. **exposure to external licensing, service, or equipment rules**;
5. **multi-generation manufacturing continuity**.

The January 2025 U.S. BIS foundry due-diligence rule broadened licensing and due-diligence requirements for front-end foundries / packaging companies handling **certain advanced-computing ICs**, and introduced the Approved / Authorized IC Designer framework. The rule uses 16/14 nm-or-below or non-planar logic among the conditions relevant to its presumption framework for certain advanced logic ICs.[^22] This cannot be simplified into "all PRC-designed smartphone SoCs below 16/14 nm automatically require a license." Applicability depends on EAR classification, performance/transistor-count conditions, designer status, destination, and transaction structure.

The defensible risk statement for UNISOC / Xiaomi is therefore:

> **External-foundry dependence increases supply-continuity and policy exposure, but without product-specific EAR classification and licensing evidence it cannot be rewritten as an already-occurring or inevitable cutoff.**

Consumer parallel-supply capability should therefore be evaluated as its own layer—not used as proof for or against frontier-AI closure.

## 3. PCs / workstations: compatibility and scale are the binding tests

PCs, workstations, and general computing require CPU/GPU hardware, DDR5, professional software, drivers/tooling, desktop ecosystems, and developer compatibility.

It is also inaccurate to describe the domestic PC CPU/GPU segment as "blank."

Loongson's current official portfolio includes desktop/general-purpose CPUs such as the 3A6000 and 3B6000.[^5] Moore Threads publicly sells the MTT S80 gaming GPU.[^6]

These facts establish **real products and deployable domestic PC/graphics systems**.

They do not establish:

- mainstream consumer-PC shipment share approaching Intel/AMD/NVIDIA;
- equivalent coverage of professional software / games;
- equivalent performance-per-watt, driver maturity, developer ecosystem, or supply scale.

A more accurate description is:

> **real products and domestic ecosystems exist, while mainstream scale, compatibility, and system economics remain the closure barriers.**

## 4. Frontier AI is a system-closure problem

Frontier AI requires multiple advanced layers to work simultaneously:

- advanced accelerators;
- HBM-class memory;
- advanced packaging;
- high-speed scale-up / scale-out interconnect;
- compiler/runtime;
- distributed training;
- rack-scale deployment;
- data-center power.

A breakthrough in one layer does not by itself create an equivalent system.

An accelerator without HBM, hardware with very high migration cost, or a cluster with weak utilization/interconnect scaling can all produce materially weaker system economics.

A domestic chip being able to run a model is therefore not sufficient evidence of frontier substitution.

## 5. Ascend / CANN should be judged by system completeness, not peak-card metrics

The key value of a domestic accelerator stack is not the peak FLOPS of one generation.

It is whether compiler/runtime, framework adaptation, distributed communication, deployment tooling, and production-workload feedback form a closed loop.

The preferred observables include:

- large-cluster utilization;
- compiler stability;
- operator completeness;
- framework compatibility;
- failure recovery;
- multi-generation compatibility;
- production deployment scale.

Because some specific Ascend/CANN performance and migration-efficiency figures currently come primarily from secondary sources, this chapter does not upgrade unverified parameters into confirmed facts.

## 6. Memory: conventional scale and frontier HBM are different thresholds

CXMT's **May 27, 2026 STAR Market registration prospectus** discloses DDR and LPDDR products. The company subsequently began trading on Shanghai's STAR Market on **July 27, 2026**.[^7][^18]

Full-text searches of the relevant public offering / registration materials for `HBM` and the Chinese term for high-bandwidth memory do not provide evidence sufficient to confirm a volume HBM product line. The evidence therefore supports only a narrow conclusion:

> **Current public offering and product materials are insufficient to confirm a volume-deliverable HBM product line.**

This does **not** prove that the company has no HBM R&D. Listing changes the company's capital-market and financing state; it does not by itself cross the HBM, advanced-packaging, or frontier-system technical gates.

To move HBM from possible R&D into frontier-system supply, the evidence would need to include official products, customer qualification, volume production, bandwidth/capacity, and packaging integration.

The same prospectus also requires strict separation between project investment and planned offering proceeds. For the DRAM technology-upgrade project, **RMB18.0B total investment** and **RMB13.0B planned offering proceeds** are different denominators.[^7]

DDR/LPDDR supply expansion and HBM-class memory closure therefore must remain separate.

### 6.1 Conventional DRAM / NAND can improve before HBM closure

CXMT's current official product page discloses DDR5 chips up to **8000 Mbps**, with 16Gb/24Gb densities and multiple form factors serving server, workstation, PC, and other computing uses.[^8]

YMTC officially describes itself as a 3D NAND IDM and discloses its Gen5/Xtacking 4.0 product path together with raw NAND, embedded memory, client SSD, and enterprise SSD portfolios.[^9]

These facts support a narrower conclusion:

> **consumer/general-purpose memory resilience can improve before frontier HBM closure is achieved.**

Front-end manufacturing for conventional DRAM and NAND is not the same physical pool as TSMC leading-edge logic foundry capacity.

But HBM-class AI systems can reintroduce advanced-logic/base-die, advanced-packaging, and accelerator-integration dependencies.

"Conventional memory can bypass TSMC logic foundry" and "the frontier AI memory stack is independent of TSMC" are therefore different propositions.

### 6.2 Revenue share and shipment share are different denominators

CXMT's later May 27, 2026 registration prospectus, citing Omdia, reports that based on **2025 Q4 DRAM revenue**, CXMT held approximately **7.67% global share**, ranking fourth globally and first in China.[^7]

The earlier prospectus reported **3.97%** for 2025 Q2.[^11] The two observations are the same revenue-share denominator at different dates and should be presented as a dated progression rather than as competing "current" figures.

YMTC's latest institutional metric uses a different denominator. Counterpoint Research's Q2 2026 Memory & Storage Tracker places YMTC at roughly **14% of NAND shipments**, ranking third globally.[^12]

Shipment share should not be treated as a revenue-share series or used to infer pricing power without a matching denominator.

What the available evidence supports is a narrower conclusion:

> **China's conventional DRAM / NAND presence in global supply has risen materially, but share growth does not by itself prove HBM, leading-edge logic, or frontier-system closure.**

Claims such as "capacity will double by 2027" or specific Phase-III monthly wafer output remain B/C-grade background unless they are disclosed directly by the company or another formal first-party source.

### 6.3 Storage resilience is broader than NAND

AI data-center storage also includes nearline HDD.

Stronger domestic NAND / SSD capability therefore cannot be rewritten as "the entire storage stack is domestically closed," because data-center storage also includes non-NAND media and workload-specific storage tiers.

AI has also not simply eliminated HDD. Mass-capacity / cold-storage demand remains real, while QLC nearline SSD is accelerating substitution for selected workloads.

## 7. Foundry revenue share is not a frontier-AI capability metric

TrendForce's 1Q26 foundry revenue-share estimates are approximately:[^14]

**Table 17.1 — Global foundry revenue share, 1Q26 (TrendForce)**

| Foundry | Revenue share |
|---|---:|
| TSMC | 72% |
| Samsung Foundry (excluding System LSI) | 6.5% |
| SMIC | 5.1% |

**Source:** TrendForce, 2026-06-12.[^14]  
**Note:** These are revenue shares among the global Top-10 foundries tracked by TrendForce, not advanced-node yield, AI wafer capacity, or frontier-system capability.

These are foundry **revenue shares**, not advanced-node yield, AI wafer capacity, or frontier-system delivery capability.

A frontier AI parallel supply stack requires more than logic dies. It also needs advanced packaging, substrates, HBM integration, yield, high-volume qualification, and power.

Node progress is a necessary condition, not the whole system.

## 8. Algorithmic efficiency can relax hardware constraints without closing the stack

The DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report shows that algorithmic and architectural design can materially lower the hardware required per unit of capability.

The paper reports **2.664M H800 GPU-hours for pre-training** and **2.788M H800 GPU-hours for full formal training**.[^15] Using the paper's US$2/GPU-hour assumption, **US$5.576M corresponds to full formal-training compute conversion**, not full R&D cost.

The supported mechanism is that in a supply-constrained environment, sparse architecture, memory efficiency, routing, quantization, and scheduling can improve effective compute productivity.

It does not support the claim that physical supply no longer matters, nor that any constrained system can reproduce the same economics automatically.

Algorithmic efficiency is a lever—not a substitute for HBM, packaging, interconnect, and power.

## 8A. System engineering can compensate for manufacturing disadvantage, but the compensation has a cost

A manufacturing-node disadvantage does not translate one-for-one into a system-output disadvantage.

A constrained stack can partially compensate through:

- sparsity, quantization, routing, and memory-efficiency gains;
- larger numbers of dies / accelerators;
- chiplet, board-level, or rack-level topology;
- more aggressive compiler / runtime co-design;
- workload-specific scheduling and communication optimization;
- narrower specialization around captive workloads.

This is a real competitive axis and prevents process-node position from being treated as a deterministic system verdict.

But compensation is not free. Using more devices, larger topologies, or more elaborate software can increase pressure on other denominators:

- logic-wafer input;
- package starts / integration complexity;
- memory capacity and bandwidth;
- interconnect and switch count;
- power and cooling;
- failure domains and operational complexity;
- capital required per delivered workload.

The correct test is therefore not “Which node is more advanced?” and not “Can software erase the node gap?”

It is:

> **Can system engineering convert the available manufacturing base into competitive production-workload economics without simply moving the bottleneck into memory, packaging, networking, power, reliability, or capital?**

Because those inputs use different physical units, they should not be collapsed into one synthetic compensation score. The relevant comparison uses bounded metrics such as throughput / cost per target workload, power per verified output, qualified package availability, memory intensity, utilization, and multi-generation continuity.

This gives engineering compensation its proper role: **it can compress a hardware disadvantage; it does not remove the need for system closure.**

---

## 9. Domestic workloads create the iteration loop

A parallel stack does not need to win the global developer ecosystem before it can create value.

A sufficiently large captive workload can generate production feedback in compiler behavior, deployment, failure recovery, operator coverage, and utilization.

The logic is similar to hyperscaler custom silicon:

> **large captive workloads can first support a narrower but complete system stack before the stack expands toward a broader external ecosystem.**

Long-run progress should therefore be evaluated from production workload scale and persistence—not benchmarks alone.

## 10. Export controls reach manufacturing continuity, not only accelerator access

Export restrictions raise the near-term cost of acquiring advanced hardware and can slow frontier-cluster deployment.

A separate upstream issue is semiconductor-manufacturing equipment, software, and service. ASML's public statements confirm that EUV-system sales are already restricted and that the most advanced immersion DUV systems require export licences under Dutch controls; later U.S./Dutch restrictions also expanded controls affecting additional semiconductor-manufacturing equipment, metrology, and software.[^16][^17]

This does not mean every domestic memory fab would immediately stop operating under a disruption.

It does mean:

> **domestic memory capacity growth ≠ upstream manufacturing autonomy**

Whether new capacity can ramp, process generations can advance, and installed tools can be sustained depends on critical equipment, software licences, spare parts, field service, and materials qualification continuity.

Over the longer run, the same restrictions increase the incentive for domestic substitution, local hardware/software integration, and parallel-ecosystem development.

Export control is therefore not a one-directional variable.

It can widen short-term performance/scale gaps while strengthening the long-run incentive to build a parallel system.

The final outcome depends on R&D efficiency, capital allocation, ecosystem adoption, and supply scaling—not policy alone.

## 11. Two closure tests

Treating "production substitution" and "full autonomy" as a single threshold creates two opposite analytical errors.

They should therefore be separated.

![Figure 11 — Production-Grade Parallel Stack vs. Autonomous Frontier Closure](figures/fig-11-china-closure-gates.svg)

**Figure 11 note:** Report closure-test framework. Production-grade substitution does not require every component to be domestic; autonomous frontier closure adds multi-generation manufacturing and infrastructure/capital continuity under constrained external inputs.

### 11.1 Production-Grade Parallel Stack

A substitute system should be considered production-grade only when the following can operate at verifiable scale:

1. accelerator / compute delivery;
2. HBM-class or equivalent memory subsystem;
3. advanced packaging / integration;
4. high-speed scale-up / scale-out interconnect;
5. compiler/runtime with acceptable migration cost;
6. frontier or high-value production workloads with sustained feedback;
7. system utilization, reliability, power, and economics.

This standard asks whether the system works commercially in real workloads.

It does **not** require every component, tool, or material to be domestic.

### 11.2 Autonomous Frontier Closure

A higher standard applies when the question is whether the stack can continue scaling and advancing when critical external inputs are constrained.

That additionally requires:

8. **advanced-manufacturing continuity:** equipment, software, materials, spares, field service, and process upgrades remain available or substitutable;
9. **infrastructure / capital continuity:** HBM, packaging, LPS/power, and long-duration capital can support multi-generation expansion.

Therefore:

> **production-grade substitution ≠ full technological autonomy**

Import dependence does not prove that no production-grade parallel stack exists. Conversely, successful production deployment does not prove autonomous frontier closure.

## 12. Why partial closure already matters globally

If China's parallel stack scales simultaneously in advanced logic, HBM, packaging, interconnect, and software, it could do more than replace imports.

It could create a second large resource-allocation center, adding foundry, memory, packaging, power, and long-duration capital demand to regional and global resource allocation.

Current public evidence is not sufficient to show that this second frontier resource center has already reached equivalent full-stack scale.

The cited May 27, 2026 registration prospectus does not disclose an HBM product line. SMIC's 5.1% figure is foundry revenue share rather than frontier capability. Multiple non-silicon layers still require independent validation.

The most accurate current state is therefore:

> **China has moved beyond the stage of "no alternative system," and a real frontier parallel stack is iterating with partial production deployment. But under this chapter's seven Production-Grade Gates, public evidence is still insufficient to confirm that a complete production-grade frontier parallel stack has closed. Autonomous Frontier Closure remains a still higher and unproven threshold.**

## 13. Current read and falsification

As of August 2026:

- smartphone / consumer computing: relatively strong parallel-supply capability;
- PC / workstation: expanding substitution, with software and professional-workload compatibility still determining closure;
- frontier AI: a real and continuing accelerator/software parallel stack exists, while HBM, advanced packaging, interconnect, software migration, power, and scale still require simultaneous validation.

The system-closure judgment should be raised if first-party evidence appears for:

- an official HBM product / revenue line;
- auditable large-cluster utilization and frontier workloads;
- scaled advanced-packaging delivery;
- multi-generation compiler/runtime compatibility;
- stable and sustainable system economics.

If these gates remain uncrossed, "a domestic technology stack exists" cannot be upgraded into "frontier-equivalent substitution."

# Part II · Chapter 18: Antitrust, Industrial Policy, and the Boundaries of AI Platform Power

## 1. System-stack power complicates relevant-market analysis

AI platform power can span accelerators, interconnect, compiler/runtime, rack reference architecture, deployment software, cloud channels, and adjacent model products.

Competitive pressure in each component market does not by itself eliminate system-level power when one platform still coordinates several critical interfaces and switching costs.

The regulatory question therefore extends beyond a single market-share number:

> **Do cross-layer interfaces, bundling, migration costs, and supply coordination create exclusionary effects together?**

## 2. Fines and remedies are different economic instruments

A fine primarily affects cash flow.

A structural or conduct remedy can alter the rules of competition.

### Fine

A monetary penalty does not necessarily change CUDA migration cost, interconnect ecosystems, rack architecture, customer workflows, or physical supply.

### Conduct / Structural Remedy

Measures that can directly alter system economics include:

- interface openness;
- interoperability requirements;
- tying / bundling restrictions;
- non-discriminatory access / supply rules;
- acquisition restrictions;
- structural separation.

The size of a possible fine is therefore not treated as the central platform-power variable.

## 3. The 10% turnover ceiling is not a fine forecast

Article 23(2) of **Council Regulation (EC) No 1/2003 of 16 December 2002** provides that certain fines shall not exceed **10% of the undertaking's total turnover in the preceding business year**.[^1]

This is a statutory ceiling.

It is not:

> **Potential fine = revenue × 10%**

Liability, seriousness, duration, relevant sales, procedural outcome, and remedy design all matter.

The 10% figure therefore indicates the strength of the legal tool; it does not enter the earnings model.

## 4. Regulatory procedure must be read by legal state

The European Commission's Article 102 materials describe the main procedural tools and legal states. This report compresses them into a **simplified monitoring map**, not an official six-step sequence:[^2]

1. complaint / ex officio investigation;
2. information gathering, market definition, and dominance / conduct assessment;
3. Statement of Objections, where issued;
4. commitment or prohibition decision;
5. fine and/or remedy, where applicable;
6. judicial review, where pursued.

These states can overlap or be skipped depending on the case; they should not be read as a mandatory linear process.

An RFI, market inquiry, or news report cannot be rewritten as "the regulator has already found a violation."

## 5. Cloud regulation is moving toward interoperability and switching

The European Commission continued a DMA cloud-computing market investigation in 2026.[^3]

On **July 1, 2026**, the Commission held a stakeholder roundtable covering cloud interoperability / technical features, financial conditions, and contractual / commercial practices. The final report is planned for **May 2027**.[^3][^4]

Regulatory attention is therefore moving beyond price toward technical interoperability, contract terms, and switching conditions.

This is directly relevant to AI system-stack competition because interfaces and contracts become competitive variables when workloads cannot migrate at low cost.

## 6. Antitrust and industrial policy optimize different objectives

Competition policy focuses on market access, tying, exclusion, interoperability, and customer choice.

Industrial and national-security policy focuses on domestic compute, semiconductor capacity, export controls, data sovereignty, and strategic autonomy.

The same government can simultaneously:

- subsidize domestic advanced manufacturing;
- promote sovereign AI / cloud capacity;
- restrict exports of sensitive accelerators;
- require greater interoperability from platforms operating in its market.

These are not inherently contradictory. They are different policy objectives.

## 7. Regulation can change four system variables

### 7.1 Interface Control

Opening compiler/runtime/network interfaces can lower migration costs.

### 7.2 Bundling / Tying

Restricting inappropriate bundling across accelerators, networks, and software can improve the feasibility of multi-vendor systems.

### 7.3 Access / Supply Rules

If discriminatory access or allocation is legally established, non-discrimination remedies can change buyer bargaining structure.

### 7.4 Acquisition / Vertical Integration

Merger control can prevent another critical intermediate layer from moving under one ownership structure.

These variables can all affect system-stack moats.

## 7.5 Reported Hugging Face transaction: merger control may extend to developer-distribution foreclosure risk

Reuters reported on August 27, 2026 (in an Aug. 26-datelined story), citing The Information, that NVIDIA had agreed to acquire Hugging Face for **US$12.9B**.[^5] No first-party NVIDIA or Hugging Face transaction announcement was identified at the report's August 28 evidence cutoff. The report therefore treats this as a **reported transaction**, not as a first-party-confirmed or completed acquisition.

If the transaction ultimately closes, the competition-policy issue is not simply that NVIDIA would own another software company. A platform already spanning accelerators, CUDA/runtime, networking, and system architecture could also control a major **discovery / distribution / tooling / deployment gateway** for the open-model ecosystem.

The merger-control question therefore becomes one of **vertical foreclosure**:

> **Could upstream accelerator/runtime control plus downstream developer-distribution control raise entry and migration costs for competing runtimes and accelerators?**

Relevant conduct variables include:

- default ranking / recommendation bias toward owned runtimes or hardware;
- higher friction for competing inference / deployment backends;
- asymmetric treatment in benchmarks, model cards, optimization artifacts, or deployment tooling;
- preferential API, metadata, download, hosting, or commercial terms;
- bundling, exclusive integration, or self-preferencing;
- whether competitors need mirrors / alternative registries merely to preserve comparable developer reach.

### 7.5.1 Regulatory boundary

The transaction report alone is not proof of illegality or monopoly:

- many Hugging Face assets can be mirrored or redistributed;
- open-weight model ownership does not by itself transfer to NVIDIA;
- multi-homing and alternative registries can constrain platform control;
- foreclosure must be shown through actual interface, ranking, access, contract, and switching-cost changes.

The higher-value antitrust evidence would therefore be **verifiable self-preferencing + rising competitor migration cost + real developer/workload concentration**, not the acquisition price by itself.

## 8. Regulation cannot create physical capacity directly

Regulation can lower switching costs.

It cannot directly manufacture:

- HBM bits;
- advanced-packaging capacity;
- N2 wafers;
- grid / energized MW.

Even highly effective interface remedies can therefore coexist with continuing physical scarcity.

Legal competition and physical supply must remain separate analytical models.

## 9. China-stack effects remain layer-specific

U.S. or European competition policy toward an AI platform does not by itself resolve China's frontier-AI constraints in advanced logic, HBM, packaging, interconnect, and production software.

Likewise, stronger domestic system capability in China does not by itself change U.S. or European antitrust analysis.

The two can influence strategic bargaining and policy, but they are not substitutes for one another.

## 10. How regulation changes S1–S4

**Table 18.1 — How regulatory variables map into S1–S4**

| Scenario | Role of regulatory variables |
|---|---|
| S1 Competitive Expansion | interface openness, interoperability, and tying limits materially reduce switching costs |
| S2 Baseline Concentration | investigations and rules persist while system structure remains broadly intact |
| S3 Resource Hegemony | remedies are insufficient to reduce multi-layer switching costs / scarcity |
| S4 Capital Hard Landing | utilization / capital clearing dominates; regulation influences post-clearing market structure |

**Source:** Report scenario framework; EU competition and DMA procedures as regulatory-mechanism background.[^2][^3]  
**Note:** The table maps mechanisms. It does not imply a final infringement finding or remedy against any specific AI platform.

Regulation is therefore not a one-directional "negative for the platform" variable.

Its structural effect depends on whether binding remedies actually reach migration, bundling, and access.

## 11. Current read

As of August 2026, regulation is an important external variable for AI system platforms.

An investigation, market inquiry, or statutory fine ceiling is not treated as proof that structural reform has already occurred.

The events that require continuous monitoring are:

- formal proceedings;
- Statement of Objections or equivalent formal complaint;
- binding conduct commitments;
- interoperability / tying / access remedies;
- final decisions;
- whether remedies actually reduce production migration cost.

Scenario weighting should change only when the legal process or remedy is sufficiently concrete to alter interfaces, access conditions, or production migration economics.

## 12. What would change the view

The importance of regulation to system-stack competition should be downgraded if:

- investigations do not progress toward formal proceedings;
- final decisions do not address interoperability, tying, access, or migration;
- remedies exist on paper but production switching costs do not materially fall;
- physical scarcity remains the dominant buyer constraint;
- market-structure change is driven mainly by technical substitution rather than regulation.

Conversely, a headline fine should not by itself increase regulatory weighting. The higher-value evidence is a binding remedy that changes interfaces, access conditions, or cross-stack migration economics.

## References

[^1]: EUR-Lex, Regulation (EC) No 1/2003, Article 23(2), https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/ALL/?uri=CELEX%3A32003R0001
[^2]: European Commission, Article 102 investigation procedure, https://competition-policy.ec.europa.eu/antitrust-and-cartels/procedures/article-102-investigations_en
[^3]: European Commission / Digital Markets Act, 2026 cloud-computing market-investigation roundtables, final report due May 2027, https://digital-markets-act.ec.europa.eu/roundtables-cloud-computing-services-2026-05-13_en
[^4]: European Commission / Digital Markets Act, stakeholder roundtable on cloud computing services, 2026-07-01, https://digital-markets-act.ec.europa.eu/commission-hosted-stakeholder-roundtable-cloud-computing-services-under-digital-markets-act-2026-07-01_en


[^5]: Reuters, “Nvidia agrees to buy Hugging Face for $12.9 billion, The Information reports,” 2026-08-27, https://www.reuters.com/technology/nvidia-talks-acquire-hugging-face-13-billion-deal-business-insider-reports-2026-08-27/

# Part III · Chapter 19: AI Infrastructure Capital Formation — Utilization, Commitments, and Duration Risk

## 1. Capital formation must be read by owner, asset life, and workload

The industry is clearly in a high-investment regime. The useful question is not whether CapEx is “high,” but **what asset is being created, who owns the risk, how long that asset is expected to earn, and whether productive workload is scaling fast enough to carry the capital base**.

Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet, and Meta do not report a single comparable "AI CapEx" measure. Their fiscal periods, PPE definitions, lease treatment, and net-purchase metrics differ.

Three separations are mandatory:

1. company-wide CapEx vs. AI-specific investment;
2. IT equipment vs. data-center / power infrastructure;
3. actual investment vs. guidance / expectation.

The analysis starts from denominator identity, then moves to duration mismatch and utilization. A mechanically summed "four-cloud AI hardware CapEx" would obscure all three.

## 2. 2026 public capital anchors

**Table 19.1 — Public 2026 capital-spending metrics for the four major clouds**

| Company | Current 2026 public metric | Identity |
|---|---:|---|
| Microsoft | calendar-2026 reported CapEx expectation ~US$175B | expectation; underlying investment expectation unchanged after lease-classification adjustment |
| Alphabet | US$195–205B | Q2 full-year guidance |
| Meta | US$130–145B | Q2 guidance; includes finance-lease principal |
| Amazon | US$169.007B | TTM PPE net purchases actual through 2026-06-30 |

**Source:** Microsoft FY26 Q4 earnings conference call; Alphabet Q2 2026 company guidance as reported by Reuters (SEC Exhibit 99.1 separately supports Q2 actual results); Meta 2026 Q2 SEC Exhibit 99.1; and Amazon 2026 Q2 Form 10-Q.[^1][^2][^3][^4]  
**Note:** Time windows and accounting treatments differ; the figures must not be mechanically added into a unified AI-hardware CapEx number.

Taken together, these public disclosures indicate that infrastructure investment remains at elevated levels.

They cannot be mechanically added because three are guidance / expectations while Amazon is TTM actual, and the accounting periods and categories differ.

## 2A. Corporate CapEx is only one funding channel

By August 2026, AI-infrastructure capital formation had expanded beyond hyperscaler corporate CapEx. NVIDIA announced memorandums of understanding with six large capital providers aimed at establishing independent compute-financing platforms and mobilizing **more than US$500B of third-party capital over time**.[^5] Separately, the PORTS-Pike structure combines secured land, power and shell (LPS), seller credit support, a **US$1.5B** NVIDIA investment in SB Energy, and a data center that SB Energy will build, own, and operate under a **20-year lease to OpenAI**.[^6]

These disclosures illustrate several financing channels that sit outside a simple hyperscaler-CapEx denominator:

- operating and finance leases;
- third-party infrastructure vehicles;
- developer-owned data centers;
- seller equity / credit support;
- long-duration customer / offtake contracts;
- power and grid investment carried on separate balance sheets.

The implication is:

> **Hyperscaler CapEx remains a critical indicator, but it is no longer the complete denominator of AI-infrastructure capital formation.**

The announced compute-financing platforms and LPS structures should be analyzed in Chapter 23 rather than forced into a single corporate-CapEx total.[^5][^6]

## 3. Duration mismatch is the core balance-sheet risk

AI infrastructure is not a single server purchase.

It combines assets with very different duration clocks:

- accelerators / servers: relatively short technology-refresh cycles;
- networking / cooling: intermediate infrastructure clocks;
- data-center buildings: long-lived assets — Microsoft extended the estimated useful lives of its datacenters and office buildings from **15 to 25 years** effective at the start of FY27;[^1]
- grid / generation / offtake: contract- and asset-specific, but often much longer-lived than compute — the Constellation / Microsoft Crane agreement, for example, is a **20-year PPA**.[^7]

**Scope note:** These categories are duration classes, not universal useful-life assumptions for every operator or project.

This creates a **duration mismatch** between technology iteration speed and infrastructure economic life.

## 4. Seller economics and buyer ROI clear on different clocks

NVIDIA's cash generation is more directly linked to inventory, capacity reservation, supply commitments, and next-generation roadmaps.

Hyperscaler capital must cover land, buildings, power, networking, custom silicon, third-party systems, and operations.

The relevant comparison is not "who has more cash?"

It is:

> **How directly can each dollar of capital improve next-generation scarce-resource access and productive utilization?**

## 5. Power couples short-lived compute to long-lived infrastructure

As rack density rises, expansion depends on more than server procurement.

It increasingly depends on grid interconnection, substations, transformers, transmission, cooling, and long-duration power contracts.

The semiconductor clock and the power-permitting clock become coupled.

When chip supply improves but energized capacity remains insufficient, the bottleneck can shift from manufacturing toward Time-to-Power.

## 6. Utilization is the first capital-cycle test

High CapEx can represent high-return expansion or overbuild.

If utilization, revenue per deployed compute, workflow volume, and customer commitments rise together, large-scale investment can remain a durable barrier.

If low utilization, weak monetization, next-year guidance cuts, commitment cancellations, and falling residual values appear together, the same CapEx base becomes a capital burden.

## 6A. Demand duration: AI inherits the digital installed base

If AI-infrastructure demand came only from frontier-model scaling, major algorithmic efficiency gains, model compression, or one large supply expansion could shorten the capital cycle materially.

But the demand pool also contains another transmission path:

> **Digital Installed Base → AI Overlay → Workflow Penetration → Automation Depth → Workload Volume → Infrastructure Demand**

Decades of IT investment have already converted large parts of enterprise activity into software, databases, APIs, digital documents, identity systems, and networked workflows. AI can add understanding, generation, prediction, decision, and execution capabilities on top of those existing digital processes without repeating the entire transformation from physical activity to digitization.

The duration of AI-infrastructure demand cannot be judged only by asking how many GPUs the next model generation requires. It also depends on:

- the enterprise digital-workflow base;
- AI penetration;
- the depth of assistant → agent / automation;
- execution frequency;
- cost per verified workflow;
- production reliability and human-review burden.

This is why **large efficiency gains and long-run growth in aggregate infrastructure demand can coexist**: lower resource intensity per task can be offset or exceeded by broader workflow penetration, higher execution frequency, or deeper agent loops.

This mechanism is not a guarantee of permanent demand. If AI fails to produce sufficiently low-cost, reliable, verifiable workflow output, the digital installed base remains potential TAM rather than realized utilization or cash flow.

## 7. Leading signals of reversal

A CapEx-cycle turn cannot be judged from stock prices alone.

This report tracks:

- **Accounting:** impairment / accelerated depreciation / write-down;
- **Utilization / Revenue:** deployed-compute utilization / AI revenue;
- **Market Clearing:** used-system prices / cloud discounts / delivery lead time;
- **Forward Commitment:** CapEx guidance / supply commitments / compute contracts / power contracts;
- **Demand Quality / Financing:** customer cash generation / third-party capital risk appetite / supplier equity and credit support / refinancing dependency.

Only broad, persistent deterioration across **multiple causally independent classes**, with cross-actor / cross-layer confirmation, is sufficient to move from "ROI pressure" toward "Capital Hard Landing." Accounting is confirmatory / severity evidence rather than a mandatory gate.

## 8. A larger capital base raises the hurdle rate for the next cycle

A distinctive feature of AI infrastructure is that the more successfully capital expands, the higher the next-stage performance hurdle becomes.

The mechanism is:

> **larger installed capital base → higher depreciation and operating cost → higher required utilization → higher monetization requirement**

AI demand can continue growing while returns on incremental capital still fall if the capital base grows faster than demand.

The more useful long-run question is broader than whether AI demand is growing:

> **Is AI-demand growth sufficient to cover AI-capital-base growth?**

That gap helps determine whether the capital cycle continues expanding, normalizes, or moves toward clearing.

---

## 9. Current read

As of August 2026, public data still supports a high-investment regime in which **capital formation remains elevated while the utilization and monetization hurdle continues to rise**, rather than a completed capital-clearing cycle.

A real turn would require multiple independent signal families across utilization/revenue, market clearing, forward commitments, demand-financing quality, and accounting to confirm broad deterioration across actors and layers. Accounting strengthens the severity assessment when present, but it is not a mandatory gate.

The larger the installed capital base becomes, the more the next stage must prove real workflows, revenue, and utilization rather than rely on the assumption that "future demand will simply be larger."

## 10. What would change the view

The view that capital expansion remains the dominant regime should be downgraded if the following persist:

- hyperscaler underlying investment expectations decline across multiple reporting periods;
- data-center / power project cancellations or deferrals rise materially;
- seller supply commitments contract;
- third-party infrastructure-capital risk appetite weakens;
- utilization and monetization lag incremental capital;
- financing costs or refinancing pressure rise materially.

A single company's accounting reclassification or one quarter of CapEx volatility is not sufficient to establish that the supercycle has ended.

## References

[^1]: Microsoft, FY26 Q4 earnings conference call, 2026-07-29, calendar-2026 CapEx expectation of approximately US$175B and datacenter / office-building estimated useful lives extended from 15 to 25 years, https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/investor/events/fy-2026/earnings-fy-2026-q4
[^2]: Alphabet, Q2 2026 earnings-call full-year CapEx guidance **US$195–205B**, company guidance reported by Reuters, 2026-07-22, https://www.reuters.com/business/google-quarterly-cloud-revenue-growth-beats-expectations-2026-07-22/ ; Alphabet SEC Exhibit 99.1 separately supports Q2 actual results, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1652044/000165204426000066/googexhibit991q22026.htm
[^3]: Meta Platforms, 2026 Q2 SEC Exhibit 99.1, FY2026 CapEx guidance including finance-lease principal, https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1326801/000162828026050596/meta-06302026xexhibit991.htm

# Part III · Chapter 20: Value Capture and Risk Bearing Across the AI Infrastructure Stack

## 1. Value capture and risk bearing are different maps

A single dollar of AI-infrastructure spending can appear as hyperscaler CapEx, system-vendor revenue, foundry revenue, memory revenue, packaging revenue, and data-center / power investment at different points in the chain. Those are accounting views of the same capital flow, not independent dollars.

The correct analytical problem is therefore not "how large is the total profit pool?" It is:

> **Which layer captures economic rent, which layer carries asset risk, and how does that balance change when scarcity, switching cost, utilization, or financing conditions move?**

This chapter maps value capture and risk bearing separately to avoid both double counting and simplistic winner/loser rankings.

## 2. Six variables determine economic rent

At minimum, the economics of a layer depend on:

1. **Scarcity:** how quickly supply can expand;
2. **Switching cost:** engineering / organizational cost of moving to an alternative;
3. **Capital intensity:** fixed capital required to sustain growth;
4. **Duration:** whether revenue, asset lives, and contract periods align;
5. **Distribution / workflow control:** whether the layer controls customers, data, and real business entry points.
6. **Financing / risk-transfer capacity:** whether the layer can reduce project financing friction, improve revenue visibility, and transfer residual-value / duration risk to capital willing to hold it.

High revenue share does not by itself imply the highest value capture, and gross margins cannot be compared across fundamentally different business models without context.

## 2A. Operating value capture and capital-market valuation run on different clocks

A company can capture strong operating economics without receiving the highest valuation multiple, and it can receive a high market valuation before those economics are fully realized.

The report therefore separates:

- **operating value capture:** revenue, gross / operating profit, free cash flow, unit economics, and cash conversion;
- **market position:** scarcity, switching cost, distribution, supply access, and bargaining power;
- **capital-market valuation:** market capitalization, equity multiple, debt pricing, and financing flexibility.

These layers interact but are not interchangeable.

A stronger market position can support higher margins and cash flow. Stronger cash flow can then improve financing capacity. A higher market valuation can lower the effective cost of equity, increase acquisition currency, support employee compensation, and expand the option set for prepayments or strategic investments.

But:

> **market cap ≠ cash flow ≠ economic rent ≠ physical resource control**

The feedback becomes strategically important only when valuation can be converted into executable financing capacity and the underlying operating economics remain credible.

This is the bridge between the value-capture map in this chapter and the **five-lens risk-adjusted-return framework** in Chapter 23.

---

## 3. System platforms monetize coordination and switching cost

NVIDIA is currently the clearest system-platform example.

FY27 Q2:

- Revenue **US$96.221B**;
- Data Center revenue **US$89.0B**;
- GAAP gross margin **75.0%**;
- GAAP operating income **US$63.734B**.[^1]

The value in this layer does not come from the accelerator die alone.

It comes from integrating compute, HBM, interconnect, software/runtime, rack architecture, and deployment tooling.

A system platform can:

- organize multiple external scarce inputs into one purchasing unit;
- create switching costs through software, fabric, and deployment;
- use strong cash generation to support next-generation supply commitments;
- coordinate roadmaps so customers can upgrade across generations with lower friction.

A system platform, however, still does not own every physical input.

Foundry, HBM, advanced packaging, and power remain external boundaries.

## 4. Foundry / packaging can control quantity without owning the highest margin

TSMC's 2025 gross margin was **59.9%**, while HPC represented **58%** of annual revenue.[^2]

The company-corrected 2025 wording was **advanced packaging slightly over 10% of revenue**,[^3] and in 2Q26 management said packaging tightness was limiting customer growth.[^4]

This shows that manufacturing value has expanded from transistor scaling into system integration.

The economic model of foundry / packaging is nevertheless different from an asset-light system platform:

> **manufacturing service + yield + utilization + CapEx execution**

A manufacturing layer can be a physical bottleneck without owning the highest margin in the whole chain.

> **bottleneck location ≠ profit location**

## 5. Memory captures scarcity rent but remains cyclical

SK hynix reported FY25:

- Revenue **KRW97.1467T**;
- Operating profit **KRW47.2063T**;
- Operating margin **49%**;
- HBM revenue more than doubled year over year.[^5]

Micron first-party evidence also shows that at the same node and same number of bits, HBM3E requires roughly **3× the wafer supply of DDR5**.[^6]

HBM value capture therefore reflects a combination of bandwidth value, manufacturing-input density, stacking / qualification complexity, and short-run scarcity.

Memory, however, remains cyclical.

If HBM capacity expands ahead of demand, generation mismatch grows, or demand slows, profit can retrace quickly.

Memory is therefore better understood as a **high-barrier manufacturing scarcity cycle** than as permanent platform rent.

## 6. Hyperscalers combine distribution power with duration risk

As documented in [Chapter 12](part-II-ch12-hyperscaler-system-stack.md), the major hyperscalers combine cloud distribution, captive / customer workloads, custom-silicon programs, and data-center scale.

For value-capture analysis, this gives them the ability to internalize part of system-platform economics without requiring this chapter to duplicate each product-level source.

They also carry:

- servers / accelerators;
- networking;
- data-center shells;
- cooling;
- power / land;
- depreciation;
- utilization risk.

Hyperscalers should therefore not be classified simply as "winners" or "losers."

Their strongest assets are distribution + workload + capital.

Their greatest risk is duration mismatch when long-duration infrastructure is built before workflow revenue materializes.

## 7. Supply commitments connect platform cash flow to manufacturing capacity

NVIDIA's disclosed manufacturing / supply / capacity commitment family increased across three reporting dates:[^7]

- **US$95.2B** on 2026-01-25;
- **US$119B** on 2026-04-26;
- **US$279B** on 2026-07-26.

The FY26 Form 10-K supports the January figure; the FY27 Q2 Form 10-Q supports the April / July sequence and states that the latest commitments are primarily related to memory and manufacturing facilities needed for data-center infrastructure systems. The underlying mix and duration are not identical across reporting dates, so this is a forward-reservation indicator rather than a perfectly homogeneous time series.

The figure can therefore be used as a **financial proxy for future resource-locking intensity**.

But the filing does not disclose a supplier-level split, so it cannot be rewritten as a specific amount "already received" by TSMC, SK hynix, Micron, or any other individual supplier.

This is an important dynamic of value capture:

> high seller cash flow can be converted earlier into next-generation wafer, memory, packaging, and component commitments.

## 7A. Financing coordination is becoming a system capability

Two August 2026 disclosures show system-platform coordination extending from supply commitments into infrastructure finance:

- NVIDIA and Apollo, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs, and KKR announced compute-financing platforms targeting **more than US$500B of third-party capital mobilization over time**; the arrangements remain subject to final agreements.[^9]
- In PORTS-Pike, NVIDIA is providing credit support for land + power + shell and investing **US$1.5B** in SB Energy; OpenAI is the customer and SB Energy will build / own / operate the data center.[^10]

A leading system platform can therefore create value not only by organizing technology and supply, but by matching:

> **long-duration workloads + LPS / compute assets + long-duration capital**

This can reduce financing friction and accelerate Time-to-Capacity.

But the same mechanism changes who bears risk.

When a seller also provides equity, credit support, or infrastructure-financing coordination, some customer-concentration, duration, and residual-value risk can move back toward the platform.

Therefore:

> **financing coordination can strengthen platform advantage, but only when analyzed together with risk transfer and workload monetization.**

The full financing and clearing mechanisms are developed in Chapters 23 and 24.

## 8. Powered land captures Time-to-Power scarcity

At approximately **120 kW of rack power consumption for an NVIDIA DGX GB rack-scale system**,[^8] data centers require stronger grid interconnection, substations, transformers, cooling, and firm generation.

Assets with powered land, existing interconnection, and executable permitting paths can capture meaningful **Time-to-Power** value.

This layer has a distinctive profile:

- slow to replicate;
- geographically fixed;
- long permitting cycles;
- contracts that often outlive a compute generation.

Power can therefore be a scarce layer, but its economics must be evaluated together with utilization and contract duration.

A long-duration PPA can be a hedge or moat in high-demand scenarios and a long-duration obligation in S4.

## 9. Model / application value depends on workflow economics

Model companies and AI applications own model capability, APIs, user distribution, and workflow entry points.

They also face open-source competition, inference/retrieval/tool costs, cloud bargaining, and rapid price compression.

Traditional SaaS margins cannot simply be transferred onto AI applications.

The correct framework is **AI Software Unit Economics**:

> Revenue or productivity value − inference − retrieval/storage − tools/APIs − human review − AI-specific cloud cost

A defensible application layer needs to convert model capability into proprietary workflows, data, distribution, retention, and measurable business outcomes.

## 10. End systems absorb residual-capacity and quantity-assurance pressure

Smartphones, PCs, game consoles, automotive, industrial systems, robotics, and selected high-reliability electronics remain important end systems and strategic platforms.

Their primary burden is not being “replaced by AI.” It is the reprioritization of shared or adjacent upstream resources.

For end systems, the relevant exposure is **residual capacity and supply assurance**, and the physical channels differ:

- smartphones / PCs are more directly exposed to selected leading-edge logic, DRAM/LPDDR/NAND, consumer GPUs, and selected packaging;
- game consoles are exposed through custom SoC/APU, GDDR/DRAM, NAND/SSD, and long-generation hardware economics, with memory procurement able to transmit into promotion, unit volume, and hardware margin;
- automotive / industrial systems are more exposed to mature memory, MCU/analog, power, qualification, and long-life supply;
- robotics connects industrial electronics with edge/cloud AI;
- defense / aerospace / space electronics are more constrained by trusted, qualified, low-volume, long-life, RF/power, rad-hard, and special-process continuity than by direct competition for the same HBM pool.

The end-system layer should therefore not receive a single universal profit ranking, and not every downstream pressure should be attributed to AI.

The more useful variables are:

> **shared-resource exposure + buyer power + qualification rigidity + supply assurance + product-mix reallocation**

Chapter 3 defines the resource mechanism; Chapter 21 develops the observed pricing, configuration, longevity, and allocation transmission.

## 11. Custom silicon redistributes platform rent before upstream demand

A simplified traditional path is:

> cloud / model buyer → system platform → foundry / HBM / packaging → equipment/materials → power/data center

When hyperscalers deploy custom silicon, part of the flow can bypass NVIDIA:

> cloud → custom system → foundry / HBM / packaging

This changes the ownership of platform profit.

It does not necessarily reduce total demand for advanced logic, HBM, packaging, or power.

System-stack substitution is therefore both a profit-allocation variable and an upstream-resource competition variable.

## 12. Value capture is scenario-dependent

Value capture changes by scenario:

- **S1 Competitive Expansion:** custom stacks, open software, and supply expansion reduce platform rents and distribute value more broadly;
- **S2 Baseline Concentration:** system platforms and scarce manufacturing retain strong value capture while cloud investment remains high;
- **S3 Resource Hegemony:** logic/HBM/packaging/power concentrate further and scarce-layer owners plus the dominant system platform gain stronger bargaining power;
- **S4 Capital Hard Landing:** buyer utilization and commitments fall, long-duration infrastructure experiences duration/ROI stress first, and upstream profit later transmits downward.

This is more robust than assigning a permanent ranking to system platforms, HBM, foundries, clouds, and applications.

## 13. Current read

As of August 2026, the strongest evidence of durable value capture in AI infrastructure is concentrated mainly in three categories:

1. **system platforms that coordinate multiple scarce layers and create switching costs;**
2. **scarce physical layers with low short-run supply elasticity and high entry barriers, including HBM, leading-edge manufacturing, advanced packaging, and powered infrastructure;**
3. **financing / coordination layers that can match long-duration workloads, LPS / compute assets, and long-duration capital.**

The third category must be analyzed together with risk transfer. If a platform provides equity or credit support, it may gain more infrastructure influence while also reabsorbing part of customer, duration, or residual-value risk.

Whether applications develop a durable profit pool still depends on workflow economics and distribution.

The right question is therefore not "How much money is being spent on AI in total?"

It is:

> **How much repeatable cash flow remains at each layer, how much non-substitutability is created, and how much long-duration risk remains on the balance sheet?**

## 14. What would change the view

The current value-capture map should be reweighted if:

- system portability improves materially and switching costs fall;
- HBM / packaging / power scarcity premiums disappear on a sustained basis;
- financing coordination fails to lower capital cost or improve utilization;
- supplier-assisted financing begins generating material credit or impairment losses;
- application-layer workflow economics improve enough to create more durable profit pools;
- upstream manufacturing develops a genuine qualified second source.

Value capture is not a permanent ranking. It is the dynamic result of scarcity, switching cost, capital intensity, duration, distribution, and risk transfer.
