# Core Concepts and Model Index

> This page defines the common analytical vocabulary used across the report. The goal is not to manufacture jargon, but to stop the same mechanism from being renamed, duplicated, or misread as an already-observed fact.

> **Vocabulary policy:** this is not a catalog of proprietary theories. Terms in this index serve three different roles:
> - **report-defined analytical constructs:** Incremental Scarce-Capacity Access, Non-AI Residual Capacity, Rolling Bottleneck Cascade, cloud system-stack substitution + parallel-stack closure boundary, Financing Reflexivity, AI Capacity Gap / Growth Gap, and AI inheritance;
> - **descriptive shorthand:** resource reallocation pressure, Super-buyer Effect, scarce-layer value capture, AI Price-Tolerance Gap, two-speed semiconductor economics, anchor-buyer mechanism, and Three-Layer AI Skills Stack;
> - **standard or imported analytical vocabulary:** crowding-out, capacity preemption, compute rent, installed-base flywheel, hysteresis, unit economics, task-to-job translation, and foreclosure pressure.
>
> Only the first group is intended to carry report-specific model structure. The other terms are navigation labels or standard analytical language and should not be cited as independent discoveries.

## 1. Resource Reallocation Pressure

**Definition:** when AI/HPC demand combines higher willingness to pay, stronger order certainty, longer contracts, and higher expected returns, scarce resources—advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, power, land, talent, and capital—tend to be allocated toward AI/HPC.

**Type:** Mechanism (M), not a market-share statistic.

**Failure conditions:** supply grows materially faster than AI absorption; AI returns fall; or technology reduces dependence on the same scarce resources.

## 2. Super-buyer Effect

**Definition:** very large AI platforms gain disproportionate influence over resource allocation through order scale, prepayments, long-duration commitments, credit quality, and roadmap certainty.

This is not “highest bidder takes all capacity.” It is a multi-variable ranking of long-term economic value to suppliers.

## 3. Incremental Scarce-Capacity Access

**Definition:** evaluate whether different buyers can repeatedly obtain sufficient, qualified, and timely supply in the next generation of scarce physical resources.

Analyze each physical layer separately:

- advanced logic;
- HBM / DRAM;
- advanced packaging;
- networking;
- land / power / shell;
- energized power.

The report does not compress these physical pools into one synthetic cross-layer percentage.

More reliable variables include:

- qualification status;
- lead time;
- long-term agreement / prepayment;
- allocation fulfillment;
- supplier diversification;
- delivery schedule;
- buyer credit quality;
- supply elasticity.

**Boundary:** revenue share, demand share, wafer share, packaging allocation, IT-GW, and generation GW are not interchangeable.

## 4. Non-AI Residual Capacity

```text
NonAI_Residual(t)
= Effective_Supply(t)
- AI_Absorption(t)
- Other_Priority_Demand(t)
```

Used to test whether capacity expansion actually restores supply to smartphones, PCs, general servers, automotive, and other non-AI industries.

## 5. Observed Crowding-out

Non-AI industries receive fewer effective resources in absolute terms, or visibly face lower specifications, delays, or materially higher prices.

This can be observed directly.

## 6. Counterfactual Crowding-out

Non-AI supply still grows, but less than it would have grown without rapid AI absorption.

This explains how “total capacity expanded” and “traditional industries still feel constrained” can both be true.

## 7. Capacity Preemption

**Definition:** reserving future capacity through prepayments, long-term agreements, capacity commitments, or first-launch scheduling.

Capacity preemption is not automatically a legally exclusive agreement. The report distinguishes:
- disclosed aggregate commitments;
- supplier-specific commitments;
- node/packaging-specific commitments;
- report-side forward inference.

## 8. Scarcity-layer Value Capture

Replaces the older phrase “margin monopoly.”

**Definition:** firms operating in scarce, difficult-to-substitute layers can earn excess profit, cash flow, and bargaining power, then reinvest those gains into the next generation of resource reservation.

High gross margin is one indicator. It is not a legal monopoly finding.

## 9. Compute Rent

**Definition:** excess value capture arising from scarcity in compute platforms, software ecosystems, hardware supply, and system integration.

Boundary:
- NVIDIA Data Center revenue is an important seller-side proxy for AI-infrastructure monetary flow;
- it is not equal to “all global compute rent.”

## 10. Installed-base Flywheel

```text
more resources
→ more shipments
→ more installations
→ more developer/customer feedback
→ stronger software and next-generation products
→ stronger cash flow and order certainty
→ stronger prepayment capacity
→ more resources in the next generation
```

This is a core dynamic in the report's system-concentration scenarios.

## 11. Rolling Bottleneck Cascade

The scarce layer does not have to remain the same for ten years.

Bottlenecks may move across:
- advanced logic;
- HBM;
- advanced packaging;
- substrates;
- networking;
- liquid cooling;
- main transformers;
- grid interconnection;
- generation capacity and long-duration power contracts.

Even if one component loosens, a downstream system can remain constrained if the bottleneck rolls to the next layer.

## 12. AI Price-Tolerance Gap

**Definition:** AI-infrastructure buyers can often tolerate a higher unit resource price and higher capital cost than many consumer-electronics or traditional-industrial buyers.

This helps explain why market-clearing allocation can tilt toward AI/HPC.

## 13. Hysteresis

Once fabs, HBM lines, packaging capacity, data centers, and power infrastructure are built around AI demand, they do not instantly revert to traditional consumer capacity if AI demand later cools.

Shortage, expansion, surplus, and recovery are therefore asymmetric processes.

## 14. Self-financing Demand Loop

A leading platform uses strong profit and free cash flow to fund expansion, prepayments, and supply commitments, allowing current market power to become future supply advantage.

## 15. Capacity Foreclosure Pressure

**Definition:** if a leading platform repeatedly obtains materially stronger quantity assurance and earlier delivery across multiple scarce layers, competitors may lose effective competitiveness through residual capacity, lead times, cost, and product-cycle delay even without being legally prohibited from buying.

This is an industrial-economics scenario concept, not a legal finding.

## 15A. Cloud system-stack substitution

**Definition:** hyperscalers do not merely replace one GPU die with one custom ASIC. They can build a system stack from chips, interconnect, compilers/runtimes, racks/pods, internal workloads, and data-center standards.

By 2026, Google Ironwood, AWS Trainium3 UltraServer, Microsoft Maia, and Meta MTIA show that system-level substitution is real.

Key questions:
- how much training and inference moves to these stacks;
- whether software migration is sustainable;
- whether they have credible scale-up / scale-out systems;
- whether dependence on NVIDIA rack-scale systems declines;
- whether they still depend on the same HBM, advanced logic, packaging, and power;
- whether internal cost advantages become external platform competition.

System-stack substitution affects **platform profit pools**. Whether those stacks avoid the same upstream resources affects **supply-chain competition**. These are separate effects.

## 15B. Anchor-buyer mechanism

**Definition:** when model companies make multi-year or decade-scale compute commitments, they become anchor customers for data-center, chip, power, and financing projects rather than merely cloud tenants.

Current anchors:
- OpenAI Stargate: 2025-01 announced a **US$500B** four-year infrastructure plan; by 2026-04 OpenAI said it had passed a **10 GW secured-capacity milestone**; the **1 GW** Michigan campus broke ground in 2026-06;
- PORTS-Pike / NVIDIA / SB Energy / OpenAI: initial **4.25 IT-GW** design capacity supported by land + power + shell (LPS) coordination and credit support, with phased delivery planned from 2028;
- Anthropic / AWS: more than **US$100B** over ten years, up to **5 GW**, with large-scale Trainium use.

Mechanism:

```text
long-duration model demand
→ greater infrastructure financing certainty
→ earlier cloud / data-center / power construction
→ more stable chip and memory orders
→ priority capacity for the model company
→ larger training and inference scale
→ stronger future demand commitments
```

This creates two-way dependency: platforms own heavy assets and physical infrastructure; model companies provide high-value demand and future utilization.

Boundary: contract value or GW commitment ≠ already energized load, chip price, actual utilization, or profit split.

## 15C. AI Software Unit Economics

AI-native software does not have near-zero marginal cost in the same way as traditional pure-software replication.

```text
AI_Gross_Margin
=
(Revenue
- Model_Inference_Cost
- Retrieval_Storage_Cost
- Tool_API_Cost
- Human_Review_Cost
- AI_Specific_Cloud_Cost)
/
Revenue
```

Owning GPUs or private racks only converts part of external API cost into depreciation, power, operations, and capital cost.

Therefore the old “SaaS 85% → AI SaaS 55%” endpoint is not an industry fact.

Key variables:
- ARPU / outcome pricing;
- calls per user;
- Agent loop depth;
- routing and caching;
- accelerator/cloud price;
- human review and tool/API cost;
- private-infrastructure utilization.

Lower gross margin does not automatically mean lower total profit if ARPU or market size rises.

## 15D. Task-to-Job Translation Layer

AI labor impact cannot jump directly from "task exposure" to "unemployment."

```text
Model Capability
→ Task Suitability
→ Workflow Redesign
→ Adoption Economics / Responsibility
→ Job Composition
→ Employment / Wage Outcome
```

- **Model Capability:** can the system technically perform the task?
- **Task Suitability:** is the task digital, evaluable, repeatable, and sufficiently structured?
- **Workflow Redesign:** did the organization actually reorganize work?
- **Adoption Economics / Responsibility:** do cost, quality, compliance, safety, and accountability permit substitution?
- **Job Composition:** which tasks inside a job are compressed, augmented, or retained?
- only then: employment, wage, and organizational outcomes.

ILO's 2025 refined index remains an empirical anchor: roughly one quarter of global jobs have some GenAI exposure, while the highest exposure category is around 3.3%; most jobs are more likely to be transformed than directly eliminated.

## 15E. Three-Layer AI Skills Stack

1. **Tool / cognitive layer:** model use, retrieval, evaluation, workflow design.
2. **Systems-engineering layer:** compilers, runtimes, multi-model scheduling, interconnect, racks, data pipelines, reliability, and cost.
3. **Physical / responsibility layer:** power, heat, packaging, yield, PPAs, sites, licenses, safety, and compliance.

The first layer scales fastest and is easiest for model capability itself to commoditize. The second governs production deployment and migration. The third expands slowest because it is constrained by physical systems and accountability.

## 15F. Two-Speed Semiconductor Economics

**Definition:** the AI Infrastructure Economy and the Traditional Device Economy share parts of the same upstream supply chain but differ materially in willingness to pay, long-duration contracts/prepayments, expected capital returns, BOM constraints, and end-market price elasticity. Incremental capital and effective supply can therefore be allocated to the two demand classes at different speeds.

The core proposition is not that consumer electronics disappear. It is:

> **AI/HPC is increasing the influence of a relatively small number of capital-intensive buyers over marginal semiconductor demand and incremental capacity formation.**

Observable variables include HPC / smartphone / PC revenue mix, advanced-process CapEx, HBM/server-memory product mix, advanced-packaging investment, consumer BOM/pricing, replacement cycles, and Non-AI Residual Capacity.

**Weakening conditions:** AI Capacity Gap converges, supply elasticity rises materially, traditional devices regain stable residual supply, or AI workload monetization/utilization fails to support the expanding capital base.

## 15G. AI Capacity Gap / AI Capacity Growth Gap

To avoid mislabeling a growth-rate difference as a stock capacity shortfall, the report separates two metrics.

### Absolute gap

```text
AI_Capacity_Gap_i(t)
= AI_Effective_Demand_i(t)
- AI_Effective_Capacity_i(t)
```

where \(i\) is measured separately for advanced logic, HBM/DRAM, advanced packaging, networking, power, and other physical layers. Demand and capacity must use the same delivery window, qualification/quality boundary, and physical unit.

**Purpose:** determine whether the layer is currently short of deliverable supply.

- **Absolute Gap > 0:** current shortage;
- **Absolute Gap ≈ 0:** near current balance;
- **Absolute Gap < 0:** current effective headroom.

### Growth gap

```text
AI_Capacity_Growth_Gap_i(t)
= Growth(AI_Effective_Demand_i(t))
- Growth(AI_Effective_Capacity_i(t))
```

The Growth Gap is a same-period difference in growth rates, measured in percentage points. It indicates whether the absolute gap is more likely to widen or converge.

- **Growth Gap > 0:** scarcity may widen, or existing headroom may erode;
- **Growth Gap < 0:** scarcity may converge, or headroom may expand;
- **Growth Gap = 0:** does not imply that no current shortage exists.

A strong S4 signal requires the Absolute Gap to turn negative or effective excess capacity to emerge while utilization, clearing prices, forward commitments, accounting indicators, and demand-financing quality deteriorate together.

AI Capacity Gap / Growth Gap are complementary to Non-AI Residual Capacity: the first two describe the AI-side current gap and its direction; NARC describes effective supply left for non-AI demand after priority allocation.

## 15H. Financing Reflexivity

**Definition:** when a system seller also uses equity investment, credit enhancement, LPS support, or compute-financing coordination to help customers and infrastructure projects obtain capital, seller-side demand growth and customer financing conditions can become mutually reinforcing.

A representative loop is:

```text
platform cash generation
→ equity / credit support
→ infrastructure financing improves
→ anchor capacity expands
→ platform-system demand expands
```

This does not mean demand is artificial.

The report distinguishes:

1. independently financed end demand;
2. long-duration Anchor Buyer commitments;
3. third-party infrastructure capital;
4. supplier equity / credit support.

If utilization and monetization are healthy, supplier-assisted financing can accelerate real infrastructure formation.

If utilization weakens while supplier support and refinancing dependency keep rising, the independence of forward-demand signals should be downgraded.


## 15I. AI inheritance

**Definition:** AI does not need to rebuild the digital economy from zero. It can inherit decades of Information Technology infrastructure—software, databases, cloud, APIs, digital documents, source code, identity/access systems, MES/ERP/CRM, and networked workflows—and add understanding, generation, prediction, decision, and execution layers on top.

**Type:** Mechanism (M), not evidence that every industry is already AI-native.

Core transmission:

```text
Digital Installed Base
→ AI Overlay
→ Workflow Penetration
→ Automation Depth
→ Workload Volume
→ Infrastructure Demand
```

**Long-run implication:** AI-infrastructure demand depends not only on frontier-model scaling, but also on how deeply AI penetrates existing digital workflows, how frequently those workflows execute, and how much automation depth they acquire. Algorithmic efficiency can reduce resources per task while total infrastructure demand still rises if workflow penetration or execution volume grows faster.

**Boundary:**
- the marginal friction of Digital → AI can be lower than Industrial → Digital, but it is not zero;
- production adoption still depends on data quality, integration, security, governance, responsibility, reliability, human review, and unit economics;
- insufficiently digitized Physical AI / industrial processes still require sensorization, control integration, qualification, safety, and field modification;
- a digital installed base represents potential convertible workload, not realized utilization, revenue, or cash flow.

## 16. Three quantities that must remain separate

1. **Total supply** — total market capacity / bit / wafer / output.
2. **AI absorption** — resources actually consumed by AI/HPC.
3. **Non-AI residual** — effective resources available to other industries after priority allocation.

Any chapter that reports only the first quantity and concludes “supply has recovered” is incomplete.

## 17. Relationship among the models

```text
Resource reallocation pressure
        ↓
Super-buyer effect / anchor-buyer mechanism
        ↓
Capacity Preemption / Financing Support
        ↓
Layer-specific Incremental Scarce-Capacity Access strengthens
        ↓
Non-AI Residual Capacity / competitor access changes
        ↓
Installed-base Flywheel
        ↓
Scarcity-layer Value Capture
        ↓
Self-financing Demand Loop / Financing Reflexivity
        ↺
```

Rolling Bottlenecks in power, packaging, HBM, and other layers determine where this loop temporarily slows, migrates, or reverses.

## 18. Research discipline

- Facts describe what has happened.
- Hypotheses use “if,” assumptions, and stress tests.
- Models define variables and causal transmission.
- Scenarios may be bold.
- Legal terminology is used only with legal support.
- Every strong claim should name at least one reverse signal.

---

# Core Hypothesis Matrix (2026–2036)

> This matrix defines forward-looking input variables only. None of the levels below should be treated as already-observed facts.  
> Purpose: force the report's future-facing judgments to use one common set of assumptions rather than mixing incompatible growth, supply, financing, and end-state assumptions.

## 1. How to use the matrix

Each scenario is a combination of H variables.

```text
Fact F
+ Hypothesis H
+ Mechanism M
→ Scenario S
→ Falsification / Update X
```

Every 2030 or 2036 conclusion should be traceable to a combination of variables below.

## 2. Core variables

| ID | Variable | Low case | Baseline case | Strong case | Extreme stress test | Main effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| H1 | AI/HPC demand growth | materially slows | remains high | stays high for multiple years | nonlinear demand acceleration | determines AI absorption rate |
| H2 | effective advanced-logic supply growth | faster than demand | close to demand | slower than demand | materially behind demand | determines leading-edge scarcity |
| H3 | incremental scarce-capacity access / concentration | multiple buyers obtain qualified supply reliably | leading buyers have clear priority but real substitute access remains | access across several scarce layers shifts toward a small set of buyers | multi-layer access remains highly concentrated with weak substitute quantity assurance | determines competitor delivery certainty and product-cycle pressure |
| H4 | HBM wafer trade ratio | improves materially | remains structurally above DDR | ~3×-class penalty persists | worsens with stacking | determines DRAM crowding-out pressure |
| H5 | advanced-packaging expansion | faster than demand | broadly matches demand | remains tight | becomes the primary bottleneck | determines deliverable AI-system volume |
| H6 | power / interconnection speed | improves rapidly | improves gradually | remains slower than data-center buildout | power becomes a hard ceiling | determines whether reserved hardware becomes usable compute |
| H7 | cloud system-stack substitution | strong substitution in training and inference | major inference diversion, partial training migration | limited internal substitution | external ecosystem and large training remain highly dependent on the dominant external stack | determines platform demand and profit-pool ceiling |
| H8 | software migration cost | falls rapidly | falls gradually | remains high | strengthens further | determines whether hardware alternatives become real substitutes |
| H9 | token / workflow unit-price decline | slow | structural decline | rapid | price war / open-source shock | affects cloud and application ROI |
| H10 | AI usage / workflow growth | slow | high | very high | explosive | jointly with H9 determines whether depreciation is covered |
| H11 | antitrust / interoperability regulation | forced opening | behavioral constraints | light regulation | little structural change | determines platform control |
| H12 | second advanced-manufacturing source | mature and scalable | limited substitute | unstable | effectively one frontier source | determines foundry concentration risk |
| H13 | China Production-Grade Parallel Stack | broad production workload coverage | selected high-value workloads operate reliably | mainly selected domestic workloads | narrow workload / limited scale | determines depth of real substitution and size of the second system stack |
| H14 | AI return on invested capital | falls materially | acceptable | very high | excess returns persist | determines direction of profit-seeking capital |
| H15 | prepayment / long-contract / capacity-reservation intensity | terms loosen | stable | strengthens | multiple scarce layers become heavily pre-reserved | determines Capacity Preemption and quantity assurance |
| H16 | non-AI demand resilience | weak | stable | strong | strong but unable to pass through price | determines social and industrial cost of crowding-out |
| H17 | long-duration model-company compute commitments / Anchor Buyer Demand | shortened or rescheduled | stable multi-year commitments | more decade-scale orders | model companies become anchor customers for infrastructure projects | determines financing certainty for cloud, chips, data centers, and power |
| H18 | supplier-assisted financing intensity | mainly customer / third-party financed | limited, transparent seller support | equity / credit support rises materially | utilization weakens while seller support keeps increasing | determines demand-signal independence and financing reflexivity |
| H19 | China Autonomous Frontier Closure | upstream continuity is strong and scalable | some layers sustainable, others externally dependent | several upstream/infrastructure gates remain externally constrained | disruption to critical external supply materially limits multi-generation scaling | determines long-run autonomous expansion capacity |

## 3. Four standard scenarios

### S1 — Competitive Expansion

Typical combination:

- H1: high growth, then gradual moderation;
- H2: supply eventually exceeds demand growth;
- H3: multiple buyers obtain qualified supply reliably;
- H5: advanced packaging loosens;
- H6: power improves;
- H7: cloud system stacks create real diversion;
- H8: migration cost falls;
- H12: a scalable second advanced-manufacturing source emerges;
- H13: production-grade parallel stacks broaden;
- H19: critical upstream continuity improves.

Result:

- AI remains enormous, but concentration weakens;
- Non-AI Residual Capacity recovers;
- leading platforms remain strong, but excess platform value capture declines;
- the industry moves toward multipolar competition.

### S2 — Baseline Concentration

Typical combination:

- H1: sustained high growth;
- H2: supply broadly catches up but often lags one cycle;
- H3: leading buyers have clear priority, while substitute buyers still obtain effective supply;
- H4: high HBM resource intensity persists;
- H5: packaging remains periodically tight;
- H6: power becomes a major constraint gradually;
- H7: cloud system stacks divert internal inference and part of training;
- H8: migration cost remains high;
- H17: Anchor Buyer commitments remain substantial;
- H18: seller support exists but remains consistent with healthy utilization and monetization.

Result:

- leading platforms retain strong value capture;
- non-AI industries can experience counterfactual crowding-out;
- bottlenecks roll across logic, HBM, packaging, and power;
- traditional industries do not disappear, but upgrade costs and quantity-assurance risk rise.

### S3 — Resource Hegemony

Typical combination:

- H1: high growth persists for multiple years;
- H2: effective supply remains below demand;
- H3: access across several scarce layers concentrates toward a small set of buyers;
- H4: HBM trade ratio stays high;
- H5: advanced packaging remains quota-like;
- H6: power becomes a hard ceiling;
- H7: cloud stacks fail to dislodge the core training / high-value workload stack;
- H8: software migration cost stays high;
- H14: excess AI returns persist;
- H15: prepayments and long contracts strengthen;
- H17: decade-scale model-company commitments expand;
- H18: financing coordination expands but is still supported by real utilization;
- H19: autonomous closure remains incomplete or only partial in important competing stacks.

Result:

- resource-allocation power concentrates;
- competitors fall behind first on product cycles and delivery certainty rather than on the ability to design a chip at all;
- Non-AI Residual Capacity remains structurally tight;
- capital formation tilts further toward AI;
- the Installed-base Flywheel reinforces itself.

### S4 — Capital Hard Landing

Typical combination:

- H1: demand materially misses expectations;
- H9: unit prices fall rapidly;
- H10: usage growth fails to offset price declines;
- H14: AI returns fall;
- H15: supply commitments slow or are rescheduled;
- H17: long-duration model-company commitments shrink, are rescheduled, or run at low utilization;
- H18: supplier equity / credit support rises while independent monetization and utilization weaken;
- H6: previously signed long-duration power and construction commitments remain.

Result:

- GPU/rack pricing and lead times soften first;
- HBM/packaging expansion slows later;
- installed hardware continues to depreciate;
- long-duration power and buildings become the hardest assets to exit;
- if clearing removes mainly the second tier, leading platforms can emerge more concentrated afterward.

## 4. How H3 should be used

H3 does **not** use one composite percentage for advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, networking, and power.

Correct use is layer by layer:

- how many qualified suppliers exist;
- whether competitors obtain effective quantities on schedule;
- whether lead time / allocation fulfillment diverges materially;
- whether long-duration commitments pre-reserve future supply;
- whether supply elasticity improves;
- whether customer concentration actually enters physical allocation.

The following substitutions are prohibited:

> revenue share → wafer share → packaging allocation → HBM share → power access

If reliable customer-level allocation data becomes available for one physical layer, a percentage may be used for that layer only. It cannot be generalized into a synthetic full-stack "resource capture rate."

## 5. Condition for writing "non-AI supply has recovered"

Only if:

```text
ΔEffective_Supply
>
ΔAI_Absorption + ΔOther_Priority_Demand
```

and the condition persists long enough for the absolute level or demand-relative shortfall of NonAI_Residual to improve materially.

Otherwise the report may say:

- total supply increased;
- the shortage narrowed;
- lead times improved;
- pricing pressure eased.

It may not automatically say "recovered."

## 6. Formal-report chapter mapping

- Chapter 4 advanced logic: H2 / H3 / H12 / H15;
- Chapter 5 HBM: H1 / H4 / H5 / H16;
- Chapter 7 Power / LPS: H6 / H17 / H18;
- Chapter 12 Hyperscaler System Stack: H7 / H8 / H3;
- Chapter 13 Anchor Buyer: H17 / H18 / H6;
- Chapter 17 China Parallel Stack: H13 / H19 / H12;
- Chapters 19–24 capital cycle: H9 / H10 / H14 / H15 / H17 / H18;
- Chapters 28–32 timeline, monitoring, scenarios, strategy, and master judgment use this matrix rather than introducing a separate probability system.

## 7. Falsification and updating

The matrix is not designed so that every possible future can be "explained." Every variable must be updateable by reality.

If we repeatedly observe:

- AI demand slowing;
- HBM / advanced-packaging supply loosening;
- a mature second advanced-manufacturing source;
- China production-grade substitution and autonomous closure improving across several layers;
- cloud system stacks migrating large production workloads;
- materially lower software migration cost;
- power expansion outpacing deployment demand;

then S3 should receive less weight.

If instead:

- supply commitments keep expanding;
- advanced packaging continues to limit customer growth;
- HBM keeps consuming higher wafer resources;
- a small set of buyers retains materially stronger quantity assurance and earlier delivery across several scarce layers;
- grid connection remains slower than data-center buildout;

then S3 should receive more weight.

If utilization, market clearing, accounting losses, forward commitments, and demand-financing quality deteriorate broadly and persistently, S4 should receive more weight.

The value of this matrix is not a fixed probability. It is a falsifiable industrial state framework for the next decade.

---

# External Research Comparison Index

> This page is not an "authority endorsement list." Its purpose is to compare this report with publicly searchable institutional research, first-party disclosures, and industry datasets, clarifying which conclusions are externally covered and which are the report's own cross-layer mechanisms and scenario synthesis.  
> **Positioning rule:** comparisons are dimension-by-dimension. This report does not claim global uniqueness, universal comprehensiveness, or superiority to specialized systems such as SemiAnalysis, IEA, Epoch AI, sell-side research, or academic datasets. Its differentiator is the combination of cross-layer synthesis, denominator discipline, claim identity, and falsifiable scenario adjudication.  
> **Retrieval and revision cutoff: August 28, 2026**

## 1. OECD — AI Infrastructure Competition

**Report:** *Competition in Artificial Intelligence Infrastructure*  
**Institution:** OECD  
**Date:** 2025  
**URL:** https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/competition-in-artificial-intelligence-infrastructure_623d1874-en.html

OECD focuses on:

- AI accelerators and compute access;
- infrastructure supply chains;
- concentration and entry barriers;
- vertical integration;
- strategic partnerships;
- competition policy.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** AI is not a pure-software market; physical infrastructure, interface control, and vertical integration can shape competition.
- **This report adds:** a deeper link from competition policy into advanced logic, HBM, advanced packaging, LPS/power, quantity assurance, Anchor Buyers, and Financing Reflexivity.
- **Boundary:** OECD's analysis of competition risks is not a legal monopoly finding against any particular company.

## 2. IEA — Energy and AI

**Institution:** International Energy Agency  
**Date:** 2025–2026  
**URLs:**

- https://www.iea.org/reports/energy-and-ai
- https://www.iea.org/reports/key-questions-on-energy-and-ai

IEA provides:

- data-center electricity baselines and scenarios;
- grid, generation, and permitting constraints;
- efficiency and demand scenarios;
- connection and infrastructure-development clocks.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** power is an independent physical constraint on AI infrastructure.
- **This report adds:** a state model separating **Contracted / Secured / Permitted / Construction / Energized / Utilized**, while keeping generation GW, PPA capacity, and IT-GW separate.
- **Boundary:** a global electricity scenario, interconnection queue, or project announcement is not evidence of usable AI load at a specific site.

## 3. Stanford HAI — 2026 AI Index

**Report:** *The 2026 AI Index Report*  
**Institution:** Stanford Institute for Human-Centered AI  
**URL:** https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report

The AI Index covers:

- model capability;
- investment and adoption;
- infrastructure;
- labor;
- education;
- policy and governance.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** AI is a combined technological, capital, infrastructure, and social variable.
- **This report adds:** a causal chain from **system stack → scarce capacity → capital formation → workload monetization → industry transmission** rather than an annual encyclopedia of indicators.
- **Boundary:** education and labor indicators calibrate adoption and exposure; they do not directly imply a universal unemployment rate or that education systems have "failed."

## 4. SIA + Deloitte — The AI Rack Semiconductor Stack

**Report:** *Powering AI: The Semiconductor Ecosystem at the Foundation of Data Centers*  
**Institution:** Semiconductor Industry Association + Deloitte  
**Date:** 2026  
**Entry point:** https://www.semiconductors.org/new-report-finds-semiconductors-account-for-95-of-an-ai-data-server-racks-value-encompassing-the-full-stack-of-chip-technologies/

The study emphasizes that an AI rack is not "one GPU." It is a semiconductor system spanning compute, memory, networking, control, and related components.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** the unit of competition and sale is moving from chip toward system / rack.
- **This report adds:** the connection from rack architecture into advanced logic, HBM, packaging, power, LPS, financing, and workload utilization.
- **Boundary:** rack BOM structure cannot directly infer global shipments, HBM consumption, or supplier allocation.

## 5. Micron — HBM Manufacturing-Input Trade Ratio

**Source:** Micron FY24 Q2 Prepared Remarks  
**URL:** https://s25.q4cdn.com/621799436/files/doc_financials/2024/q2/FY24-Q2-Prepared-Remarks.pdf

Core first-party fact:

> Under **same technology node / same number of bits** conditions, HBM3E requires approximately **3× the wafer supply of DDR5**.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** HBM expansion changes manufacturing input, not only selling price.
- **This report adds:** the connection from the trade ratio into Non-AI Residual Capacity, the Two-Speed Semiconductor Economy, and memory product-mix rebalancing.
- **Boundary:** 3× is not a price multiple, not a permanent constant for every generation, and not a direct HBM wafer-share estimate.

## 6. TrendForce — HBM, CoWoS, Allocation, and Consumer Transmission

The report uses TrendForce mainly as **B-grade institutional research**, not as company first-party disclosure.

Key observations include:

- 2026E HBM + RDIMM at roughly 51% of DRAM bit supply;
- NVIDIA estimated above 60% of 2025 CoWoS demand;
- memory allocation fulfillment and quantity assurance;
- 2026 smartphone / PC memory-price pressure;
- moderation in mobile-DRAM price growth later in 2026.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** AI/server demand is changing memory and packaging product mix and allocation.
- **This report adds:** the integration of these signals into **AI Resource Gravity, Non-AI Residual Capacity, Quantity Assurance, and Rolling Bottleneck Cascade**.
- **Counterevidence value:** moderating mobile-DRAM price growth shows that resource pressure is not mechanically one-way; inventory, demand elasticity, and supply response still matter.
- **Boundary:** TrendForce demand estimates cannot be upgraded into TSMC or memory-vendor official customer allocation.

## 7. TSMC — Advanced Manufacturing, Packaging, and Geographic Expansion

Primary sources:

- 2025 Annual Report: https://investor.tsmc.com/static/annualReports/2025/english/index.html
- 2026 Q2 results: https://investor.tsmc.com/english/quarterly-results/2026/q2
- 2026 Q2 transcript: https://investor.tsmc.com/english/quarterly-results/2026/q2
- Arizona: https://www.tsmc.com/static/abouttsmcaz/index.htm

External anchors:

- 2Q26 HPC platform revenue mix 66%;
- advanced technologies at 7 nm and below: 77% of wafer revenue;
- 2026 capital budget: US$60B–64B;
- advanced-packaging tightness still limits customer growth;
- Arizona expansion extends beyond logic fabs into advanced packaging and R&D.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** HPC is an important marginal driver of advanced-manufacturing capital formation, while packaging is an independent constraint.
- **This report adds:** the strict separation of **revenue share ≠ wafer share ≠ allocation share**, and **geographic redundancy ≠ supplier redundancy**.
- **Boundary:** these facts do not prove any specific customer's N2 or CoWoS allocation.

## 8. ASML / Intel — High-NA EUV from Qualification into HVM

**Source:** ASML, 2026-07-15  
**URL:** https://www.asml.com/en/news/press-releases/2026/high-na-euv-reaches-new-readiness-milestone

Public anchors:

- selected Intel 18A layers have dual-qualified High-NA EUV;
- High-NA has entered high-volume manufacturing for selected Panther Lake / Intel Core Ultra Series 3 production.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** High-NA is no longer only an R&D or qualification technology.
- **This report adds:** equipment capability as part of **system-level manufacturing continuity / redundancy**.
- **Boundary:** the source does not show TSMC N2 using High-NA or all Intel 18A layers depending on it.

## 9. OpenAI / Anthropic — Anchor Compute Buyers

### OpenAI Stargate

- 2025-01: https://openai.com/index/announcing-the-stargate-project/
- 2026-04-29: https://openai.com/index/building-the-compute-infrastructure-for-the-intelligence-age/
- 2026-06-01: https://openai.com/index/stargate-michigan-data-center/

Public anchors:

- four-year US$500B infrastructure plan;
- by 2026-04, OpenAI said it had passed the **10 GW secured-capacity milestone**;
- the Michigan 1 GW campus entered construction.

### Anthropic / AWS

- https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-amazon-compute?lang=us

Public anchors:

- ten-year commitment above US$100B;
- up to 5 GW of capacity;
- more than 1M Trainium2 chips already in use.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** model companies can shape infrastructure through long-duration commitments.
- **This report adds:** the **Anchor Compute Buyer Effect**, linking demand visibility to cloud, chip, HBM, power, and financing.
- **Boundary:** secured / committed GW is not energized / utilized GW.

## 10. NVIDIA — System Coordination and Infrastructure Finance

Primary sources:

- FY27 Q2 results: https://nvidianews.nvidia.com/news/nvidia-announces-financial-results-for-second-quarter-fiscal-2027
- FY27 Q2 10-Q: https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1045810/000104581026000075/nvda-20260726.htm
- Compute financing platforms: https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Partners-With-Apollo-BlackRock-Blackstone-Brookfield-Goldman-Sachs-and-KKR-to-Establish-AI-Compute-Infrastructure-Financing-Platforms-to-Mobilize-Over-500-Billion-of-Third-Party-Capital/default.aspx
- PORTS-Pike: https://investor.nvidia.com/news/press-release-details/2026/NVIDIA-Guarantees-SB-Energys-PORTS-Pike-Technology-Campus-in-Ohio-to-Exclusively-Host-NVIDIA-AI-Compute/default.aspx

Public anchors:

- FY27 Q2 Data Center revenue US$89.0B;
- GAAP gross margin 75.0%;
- manufacturing / supply / capacity commitments US$279B;
- >US$500B third-party-capital mobilization target;
- PORTS-Pike initial 4.25 IT-GW design capacity with LPS credit support.

Relationship to this report:

- **Shared ground:** the system seller is moving from product and supply coordination into infrastructure coordination.
- **This report adds:** **Financing Reflexivity**, separating independently financed demand, Anchor Buyer commitments, third-party capital, and supplier support.
- **Boundary:** >US$500B is a mobilization target, not funded/deployed capital; 4.25 IT-GW is not current energized load.

## 11. China's Parallel Stack — External Data Must Be Read Layer by Layer

The report primarily uses:

- CXMT / Shanghai Stock Exchange;
- YMTC official materials;
- Counterpoint;
- TrendForce foundry data;
- ASML export-control materials;
- DeepSeek technical reports;
- company product pages.

Current external evidence supports:

- materially stronger domestic capability in consumer/conventional memory and selected compute;
- production-grade substitution that can no longer be described as "no domestic system exists";
- the need to keep conventional DRAM / NAND, smartphone SoC, and foundry revenue share separate from autonomous frontier closure.

The report therefore uses two levels:

1. **Production-Grade Parallel Stack**
2. **Autonomous Frontier Closure**

The second level additionally requires equipment / materials / software / service continuity and cross-generation infrastructure scaling.

## 12. ILO — Task Exposure Is Not Job Loss

**Report:** *Generative AI and Jobs: A Refined Global Index of Occupational Exposure*  
**Institution:** International Labour Organization  
**Date:** 2025

External anchors:

- roughly one quarter of global jobs have some GenAI exposure;
- the highest exposure category is around 3.3%;
- most jobs are more likely to be transformed than directly eliminated.

The report uses:

```text
Model Capability
→ Task Suitability
→ Workflow Redesign
→ Adoption Economics / Responsibility
→ Job Composition
→ Employment / Wage Outcome
```

No exposure percentage should be converted directly into an unemployment forecast.

## 13. Rules for Using External Research

External research can support:

- historical / current facts;
- institution-owned forecasts;
- industry definitions;
- competition and technical structure;
- counterevidence;
- scenario calibration.

It cannot be upgraded directly as follows:

- institutional forecast → company actual;
- demand share → allocation share;
- revenue share → wafer share;
- secured capacity → energized load;
- high concentration → legal monopoly;
- model capability → job loss;
- production-grade substitution → autonomous frontier closure.

## 14. The Report's Cross-Layer Research Question

Public research can separately cover GPUs, HBM, advanced packaging, data centers, power, CapEx, and labor.

This report focuses on the transmission among them:

> **AI high-value demand → layer-specific scarce-capacity access → system coordination → capital formation / financing → Non-AI Residual Capacity → industry transmission → utilization / monetization → next-cycle capital allocation**

The variables that require continuous validation are:

- which scarce layers are tightening or loosening;
- who receives quantity assurance;
- whether system substitution creates production-scale economics;
- whether financing increasingly depends on sponsor / seller support;
- whether traditional markets regain residual supply;
- which reverse signals are strong enough to change S1–S4 weighting.
